12 Benchmarking

Lecture slides for this chapter: Part 1 · Part 2

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

How can ML systems be compared fairly when hardware, models, data, and deployment all interact?

Every optimization claim must eventually face deployment-representative conditions, and many do not survive the encounter. Each decision targets one dimension (latency, accuracy, throughput, or energy), but an ML system is the product of all these dimensions simultaneously: a pruned model may run faster on one accelerator yet slower on another, while a larger batch size improves accelerator utilization at the cost of violating a latency service-level agreement. Similarly, an edge device may advertise peak throughput that thermal throttling sharply reduces within minutes of sustained load, and a speedup measured on a warm, idle machine with synthetic inputs can vanish or reverse once real requests, cold caches, and competing jobs share the hardware. The challenge is not whether a local metric improves but whether the combined system improves under conditions that actually matter. Benchmarking makes such comparisons systematic rather than anecdotal. It requires defining what to measure, at what granularity (a single kernel, a full model, an end-to-end pipeline), and under which conditions (batch size, input distribution, thermal state, concurrent load). Without this structure, teams compare numbers that were never measured on the same terms, and decisions that looked sound in a spreadsheet collapse under production workloads. Choosing hardware, a model, or a deployment configuration on such numbers can commit an organization to millions of dollars of infrastructure that delivers a fraction of the capacity it was bought to provide. Benchmarking validates each optimization, bringing claims into contact with evidence and quantifying the gap between promise and delivery. In D·A·M terms, benchmarking holds co-design to account by revealing whether Data, Algorithm, and Machine were matched or merely assembled.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain the benchmark–production gap and benchmarking as D·A·M validation that tests whether optimization claims hold under representative conditions
  • Design a benchmark that turns an optimization claim into a test by fixing its task, data, model, metrics, harness, and run rules
  • Select micro, macro, or end-to-end granularity based on the engineering decision being tested, and compare a roofline prediction with a measurement
  • Compare training and inference benchmarks using standardized run rules, time to quality, throughput, and latency percentiles
  • Measure energy per inference and average power with matched measurement boundaries, windows, and duty cycles
  • Determine whether a measured difference exceeds noise using repeated runs, paired tests, and confidence intervals
  • Evaluate model and data quality with confidence calibration, robustness, representativeness, and slice-level metrics, and diagnose benchmark–production gaps caused by drift, thermal throttling, and dynamic load
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

12.1 The Benchmark–Production Gap

A model quantized to INT8 may benchmark 2\(\times\) faster on a synthetic workload and show no improvement under real traffic with variable input sizes and concurrent requests. A pruned model may hold its accuracy on the test set and fail on the edge cases the benchmark never covered. Every engineering claim about speed, size, or energy is a number measured somewhere: on a warm accelerator or a cold one, at a batch of 256 or a batch of one, on curated test images or on whatever a camera returns at night. The benchmark–production gap is the difference between how a machine learning system performs under the conditions a measurement fixed and how it performs under the conditions it meets in deployment. Benchmarking is the engineering discipline that measures this gap before a deployment pays for it, and that narrows it by making the measured conditions match the deployed ones.

Hardware Acceleration ended on predictions. Its roofline placed ResNet-50’s layers against an accelerator’s compute and bandwidth ceilings, and at INT8 the doubled ridge point moved some of those layers off the compute roof. Before it, Model Compression produced an INT8 artifact a quarter of the FP32 size, and Data Selection reached target accuracy on fewer examples. Each is a favorable local number, computed for one stage in isolation, and the stages interact once they run together on a real machine under real load. Benchmarking measures which of those numbers survive. It comes before serving and operations because every later decision, from how many accelerators to provision to when to roll a model back, rests on measurements made the way this chapter prescribes.

Definition 12.1: Machine learning benchmarking

Machine learning benchmarking is the empirical measurement of ML workloads under specified conditions, used to test claims about components, models, or end-to-end systems against representative evidence rather than peak specifications alone.

  1. Significance: The gap between peak and sustained performance can be large. An H100 delivers 989 TFLOP/s of dense FP16 arithmetic at peak, yet ResNet-50 training at about 5,000 images/s per accelerator, the rate NVIDIA’s 64-accelerator H100 system logged in MLPerf Training v3.1 (MLCommons 2023), sustains about 123 TFLOP/s of model arithmetic. That is a model FLOPs utilization (MFU, achieved model FLOP/s divided by the hardware’s peak FLOP/s) of about 12.4 percent. Memory-bound layers, the input pipeline, and launch and synchronization overhead account for the difference. Benchmarking quantifies the actual \(\EfficiencyHw\) gap for a workload; vendor spec sheets do not.
  2. Distinction: ML benchmarking spans multiple scopes. Micro-benchmarks isolate operations such as matrix multiplication, model-level benchmarks evaluate complete training or inference workloads, and end-to-end benchmarks include surrounding work such as data loading, preprocessing, optimization, checkpoint I/O, or serving infrastructure. The scope must match the engineering claim.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that benchmark numbers are stable references. Both the workload (new model architectures) and the hardware (new GPU generations) evolve, so a result that leads a benchmark under one version often becomes the baseline under a later version. Year-over-year comparisons are therefore meaningful only when the benchmark version is held constant.
MLCommons. 2023. MLPerf Training V3.1 Results.

The iron law predicts how long a workload should take, and a benchmark measures how long it does; the difference between the two is the benchmark–production gap made quantitative. For bandwidth-bound workloads, the gap between peak and sustained performance follows from the memory wall. Compute-bound workloads can approach peak, yet ResNet-50’s training step shows how much of a nominally compute-bound workload still falls outside the arithmetic. Every later section that quotes a peak number is measured against this gap.

↩︎ The iron law, whose utilization term \(\EfficiencyHw\) benchmarks measure against peak, is established in Iron Law of ML Systems.

Systems Perspective 12.1: The fallacy of peak performance
A long-standing computer architecture quip calls peak performance “the performance the manufacturer guarantees you will not exceed.” For ML systems, the gap can be wide because of the memory wall. A bandwidth-bound model leaves much of the advertised FLOP/s unreachable no matter how fast the arithmetic units run. Standardized benchmarks like MLPerf require systems to run specified models and data, revealing sustained performance at a documented operating point rather than a universal rate for every workload.

ML benchmarking operates across three interdependent dimensions that map directly to the components of any deployed system. System benchmarking measures whether the hardware delivers promised performance under realistic workloads or whether memory bandwidth saturation and software dispatch overhead erode the gains. Model benchmarking measures whether optimization techniques preserve model quality across the full input distribution, not just on curated test sets. Data benchmarking measures whether the model generalizes to real-world data with all its noise, bias, and distributional shift. Each dimension can independently reveal problems invisible to the others, and a system that passes all three provides far stronger deployment confidence than one evaluated along any single axis. The three dimensions do not receive equal space. System benchmarking carries most of the chapter, because its measurement problems (how a benchmark is built, at what granularity it measures, which standardized suite it follows, and how it measures training, inference, and power) also shape how models and data are evaluated. Model and data evaluation follow once that machinery is in place, and the summary asks whether all three survive live traffic.

Systems Perspective 12.2: Benchmarks as moving targets
In traditional systems (for example, SPEC CPU), the benchmark is a rigid specification. A sorting algorithm is correct if it sorts the list. Correctness is absolute and unchanging. In ML systems, the benchmark is a soft specification: correctness is defined by a finite set of examples (ImageNet), and the world moves. A model that scores highly on ImageNet can still underperform on user photos taken years after the benchmark was created.

In computer architecture, engineers design for the benchmark because the benchmark represents the workload. In ML engineering, designing solely for the benchmark is overfitting. Robustness comes from acknowledging that the benchmark is only a proxy for a shifting reality.

Three lighthouse workloads carry the chapter, each chosen because it sits in a home regime and arrives with claims from the preceding chapters. ResNet-50, the book’s compute-bound lighthouse, carries the measurement and training half. It is the MLPerf Training reference workload, with a published quality target (75.9 percent top-1 on ImageNet) and a published per-accelerator throughput, and its INT8 profile is the one Model Compression modeled and Hardware Acceleration placed on the roofline. MobileNetV2, compute-bound in its efficient regime, carries the deployment half. It runs on one edge board in a labeled composite scenario, a wildlife camera trap whose low-power host CPU and attached INT8 accelerator must classify each frame within its deadline on battery power. The measurements made on that board recur from the harness through the energy budget. Keyword spotting (KWS), the power-bound lighthouse, carries the power section, where the question is the energy a wake-word detector spends listening rather than how fast it answers. Table 1 lists each workload’s operations and bytes, the quantities every later benchmark either confirms or refutes.

The table already separates the halves of the chapter. ResNet-50 does 13.7× MobileNetV2’s arithmetic per image, so its training benchmarks are about whether an accelerator sustains its rate; MobileNetV2’s INT8 weights fit in a few megabytes, so its deployment benchmarks are about latency, sustained thermal state, and energy on a small board.

↩︎ MobileNetV2, the lighthouse model for edge latency, is established in Depthwise separable convolutions.

Lighthouse 12.1: MobileNetV2 deployment validation
MobileNetV2 serves as the lighthouse example for validating the complete optimization pipeline. MobileNetV2 refines v1’s depthwise separable design with inverted residuals and linear bottlenecks while maintaining a similar parameter scale (Sandler et al. 2018). It exemplifies the deployment challenges where benchmarking determines success or failure: compression can reduce model size, hardware acceleration can reduce inference latency, and only benchmarking can determine whether those gains survive the full deployment pipeline.

  1. Model compression (Model Compression): INT8 quantization reduces this MobileNetV2 worked example from 14 MB to 3.5 MB (4× compression)
  2. Hardware acceleration (Hardware Acceleration): in this illustrative scenario the board’s attached INT8 accelerator runs inference in 2 ms against 15 ms on its host CPU
  3. Benchmarking validation: Verify the pipeline delivers in practice

A systematic evaluation isolates the accelerator’s latency from preprocessing and data transfer overhead, confirms INT8 quantization preserves accuracy on edge cases such as unusual lighting, and verifies that throughput holds on the camera trap’s own frames rather than on curated ImageNet test images alone.

Each stage of that pipeline arrives with a favorable number from its own chapter, such as a smaller model file or a faster kernel. The benchmarking question is which of those local numbers still hold once the stages run together on the target device, and three principles guide how the chapter answers it.

First, benchmarks are proxies, not truth. Every benchmark measures specific conditions that may not match the target deployment. A system can achieve high sample throughput in Offline mode (bulk throughput with all inputs available) and much lower queries per second (QPS) in Server mode (latency-constrained requests arriving over time). The critical question is always what the benchmark does not measure.

Second, Goodhart’s Law governs benchmark rankings.1 “When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure.” Teams that optimize for benchmark rankings often produce systems that excel in evaluation but fail in production. Benchmark-specific optimizations frequently degrade characteristics that matter for deployment: robustness, calibration, and efficiency.

1 Goodhart’s law: Goodhart (1984) articulated the original 1975 Bank of England observation on monetary policy; Strathern (1997) generalized it into the form quoted above. The original context was macroeconomics: once a monetary aggregate became an official policy target, banks changed behavior to game the metric, destroying its predictive value. In ML, the same failure mode recurs structurally: BLEU rewards n-gram overlap (Papineni et al. 2002), ImageNet rewards performance on a fixed visual distribution (Deng et al. 2009; Recht et al. 2019), and benchmark leaderboards can incentivize test-set-specific tuning.

Goodhart, Charles A. E. 1984. “Problems of Monetary Management: The UK Experience.” In Monetary Theory and Practice. Palgrave. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-349-17295-5_4.
Strathern, Marilyn. 1997. “‘Improving Ratings’: Audit in the British University System.” European Review 5 (3): 305–21. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1234-981X(199707)5:3<305::AID-EURO184>3.0.CO;2-4.
Deng, Jia, Wei Dong, Richard Socher, Li-Jia Li, Kai Li, and Li Fei-Fei. 2009. “ImageNet: A Large-Scale Hierarchical Image Database.” 2009 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 248–55. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2009.5206848.
Recht, Benjamin, Rebecca Roelofs, Ludwig Schmidt, and Vaishaal Shankar. 2019. “Do ImageNet Classifiers Generalize to ImageNet?” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 5389–400.

Third, end-to-end beats component metrics. A component speedup is diluted by every stage of the request it does not touch, and the dilution follows Amdahl’s law; section 12.3 quantifies it on a vision pipeline. These principles reappear throughout the methodology and return as misconceptions in section 12.10.

Two terms recur from here on. A service-level objective (SLO) is the target a deployed service commits to meet for a measured quantity, such as answering 99 percent of requests within a stated time; section 12.6.2.1 defines it formally. The 99th-percentile latency (p99) is the response time that 99 percent of requests meet or beat, so it describes the slowest 1 percent of requests rather than the typical one.

The chapter builds the measurement before it applies it (table 2). Benchmark carpentry turns a claim into a test that fixes the task, data, model, metrics, harness, and run rules. Granularity decides how much of the system a test spans, from one kernel to the whole request path, and it is where the roofline’s prediction for ResNet-50 meets a measurement. Benchmark suites standardize those choices across organizations. The training and inference sections apply them to ResNet-50 and to MobileNetV2 on its edge board, and power measurement adds the joules, with keyword spotting as the case. Best practices supply the statistics that decide whether a measured difference is real, and model and data evaluation test the two dimensions that a hardware result cannot certify. The fallacies and the summary close by asking whether the evidence survives production traffic.

Knowing what to measure is only half of the problem. Measuring incorrectly, with the wrong workload, a biased baseline, or an uncontrolled variable, produces numbers that look precise and mislead the decision they inform. The history of computing benchmarks2 is full of technically sound metrics applied with flawed methodology, from compiler-gamed Whetstone scores to throughput claims that predict nothing about sustained workloads (section 12.4.1). The defense is construction. A benchmark must be built so that it measures what its claim says, and that construction is where the chapter begins.

2 Benchmark: From surveying, where a “bench mark” was a horizontal cut in stone serving as a fixed elevation reference. The term entered computing in the 1970s to describe standardized comparison points, and the surveying metaphor carries a systems lesson. Just as an elevation measurement is meaningless without a calibrated reference, an ML throughput number is meaningless without controlled workloads, thermal state, and precision settings.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the three-dimensional ML benchmarking framework, what distinct failure mode does system benchmarking isolate compared to model and data benchmarking?

    1. Whether hardware accelerators, memory subsystems, and software runtimes deliver expected computational throughput and latency under workload execution patterns
    2. Whether model compression techniques preserve confidence calibration and accuracy on rare edge cases
    3. Whether the training dataset contains sufficient coverage, demographic balance, and resistance to covariate drift
    4. Whether human labeling errors and noisy annotations degrade model convergence rates

  2. True or False: Because ML benchmarks provide standardized datasets and metric formulas, a top-ranking benchmark score represents a permanent, universal verification of a model’s operational capability in production.

  3. When benchmarkers compute useful model FLOPs per elapsed second and divide by the matching numerical-path peak throughput of the hardware, the resulting metric is abbreviated as ____.

  4. Explain how Goodhart’s Law applies to ML systems benchmarking, and describe a concrete scenario where optimizing exclusively for a benchmark metric degrades real-world deployment quality.

See Answers →

12.2 Benchmark Carpentry

A claim that ResNet-50 runs faster at INT8 than at FP32 can reverse depending on whether timing starts at a JPEG on storage or at a preprocessed tensor in accelerator memory, or whether the quantized weights drop accuracy below the target floor. Benchmark carpentry is the craft of building a benchmark that measures what its claim says. It fixes the task, data, model, metrics, harness, system specification, and run rules so that the number the benchmark produces answers the engineering question and no other. A synthetic matrix-multiplication loop, a ResNet-50 training run over ImageNet, and a full serving pipeline all need these specifications, and changing one component changes what the next can measure. The components follow the D·A·M taxonomy. The problem definition fixes the input representation (Data). The dataset and task objective bound the feasible models (Algorithm). The target hardware’s memory, energy envelope, and deadlines bound how the model may execute (Machine).

Evaluating an ML system requires coupling statistical task metrics with physical efficiency metrics. Quantization and pruning reduce parameter footprint and memory bus traffic, but they alter numerical representations and risk degrading task accuracy. A benchmark that records execution latency without statistical accuracy (such as area under the ROC curve (AUC) or classification error) rewards aggressive approximations that produce invalid outputs. Conversely, reporting accuracy without latency or memory consumption conceals whether the model satisfies the target system’s physical constraints.

The measurement harness and run rules provide the experimental controls that make these evaluations reproducible. The protocol follows the question being tested. A controlled steady-state experiment can use warm-up iterations, fixed processor clocks, and pinned host memory to isolate execution differences. A deployment benchmark instead retains the relevant dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) policy, cold starts, and competing workloads, documenting their effects. Recording tail latency distributions (p95, p99) alongside the mean exposes delays hidden by averages. Each component is built here on the claim the chapter inherited, that ResNet-50 runs faster at INT8 than at FP32, and MLPerf Tiny’s anomaly-detection benchmark at the end shows every component fixed at once on a small embedded model.

12.2.1 Turning a claim into a test

Every benchmark begins as a claim. The claim from the preceding chapters, that ResNet-50 at INT8 runs faster than at FP32 without unacceptable accuracy loss, is not yet testable, because it names neither the inputs, the output, nor the conditions under which faster must hold. Fixing those boundaries is the first act of carpentry. Without an explicit task specification, hardware and software optimizations cannot be evaluated on equivalent terms: altering an input resolution, a numerical datatype, or a latency deadline changes how much work the machine does. For ResNet-50 the input is a decoded \(224{\times}224\) RGB image and the output is one of a thousand ImageNet class labels. Whether the task is natural language processing (Hirschberg and Manning 2015) or computer vision (Everingham et al. 2009; Lin et al. 2014), a complete problem definition establishes three specifications: an input specification, an output specification, and a performance specification.

Hirschberg, Julia, and Christopher D. Manning. 2015. “Advances in Natural Language Processing.” Science 349 (6245): 261–66. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aaa8685.
Everingham, Mark, Luc Van Gool, Christopher K. I. Williams, John Winn, and Andrew Zisserman. 2009. “The Pascal Visual Object Classes (VOC) Challenge.” International Journal of Computer Vision 88 (2): 303–38. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11263-009-0275-4.

The input and output specifications fix the data each run must move and define the exact scope of timed execution. The input specification fixes tensor dimensions, batching behavior, numerical representations (such as FP16 or INT8), and ingestion rates. For the ResNet-50 claim, it decides whether timing starts at a JPEG on storage or at a preprocessed tensor already in accelerator memory, a choice that can change the measured time more than the precision change being tested. Conversely, the output specification defines the termination boundary of the computation. Benchmarks frequently introduce measurement bias by timing only accelerator forward passes while ignoring the CPU post-processing required to turn raw tensor outputs into usable decisions. A robust output specification defines whether the system emits raw logits, intermediate embeddings, or final class labels, accounting for any downstream thresholding or reduction kernels that consume host memory bandwidth.

The performance specification defines the operational envelope that governs whether an optimization is viable. Unconstrained throughput numbers are meaningless if statistical accuracy degrades below acceptable thresholds, and high average throughput cannot rescue a system that violates a latency service-level agreement (SLA). For the ResNet-50 claim, the performance specification couples a quality floor (MLPerf Training requires 75.9 percent top-1, and an INT8 deployment might permit at most one percentage point of loss against FP32) with hardware execution limits. These are a tail-latency ceiling, sustained throughput matching the arrival rate of images, and a resident memory footprint that leaves room for activations at the chosen batch size. By defining these three boundaries up front, the benchmark ensures that the later stages, from dataset construction to measurement harness configuration, evaluate system optimizations against the true operational constraints of deployment.

12.2.2 Task and dataset

Fixing ResNet-50’s quality floor at 75.9 percent top-1 accuracy means nothing until the evaluation dataset itself is fixed, because a team that tests its INT8 model on a private slice of easy daytime photographs can claim no accuracy loss while a team testing on all 50,000 ImageNet validation images sees a regression. Standardized datasets enforce identical evaluation conditions across competing approaches, preventing teams from cherry-picking private slices that obscure comparative weaknesses. In computer vision, ImageNet (Deng et al. 2024, 2009), COCO (Lin et al. 2014), and CIFAR-10 (Krizhevsky 2009) establish reference tasks across classification and detection. In natural language processing, SQuAD3 (Rajpurkar et al. 2016), GLUE4 (Wang et al. 2018), and WikiText (Merity 2016; Merity et al. 2016) fulfill equivalent roles by standardizing comprehension, classification, and language modeling across diverse textual domains.

Lin, Tsung-Yi, Michael Maire, Serge Belongie, James Hays, Pietro Perona, Deva Ramanan, Piotr Dollár, and C. Lawrence Zitnick. 2014. “Microsoft COCO: Common Objects in Context.” In European Conference on Computer Vision (ECCV). Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-10602-1_48.

3 SQuAD (Stanford question answering dataset): Introduced in 2016 with more than 100,000 question-answer pairs from Wikipedia (Rajpurkar et al. 2016). AI systems exceeded the SQuAD 1.1 human baseline of 91.2 percent F1 by 2018 (Devlin et al. 2019), but the “superhuman” result reflects a benchmarking failure mode. The task’s extractive format (answers are text spans within the passage) makes it easier than open-ended question answering, inflating perceived capability relative to production NLP systems.

Rajpurkar, Pranav, Jian Zhang, Konstantin Lopyrev, and Percy Liang. 2016. “SQuAD: 100,000+ Questions for Machine Comprehension of Text.” Proceedings of the 2016 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 2383–92. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/d16-1264.

4 GLUE (general language understanding evaluation): Introduced in 2018 as a broad language-understanding benchmark (Wang et al. 2018), GLUE was quickly saturated by systems such as BERT (Devlin et al. 2019). Once GLUE became a target, leaderboard optimization reduced its discriminating power, as Goodhart’s Law predicts. The pattern motivated harder follow-on evaluations such as SuperGLUE and BIG-bench.

Wang, Alex, Amanpreet Singh, Julian Michael, Felix Hill, Omer Levy, and Samuel R. Bowman. 2018. “GLUE: A Multi-Task Benchmark and Analysis Platform for Natural Language Understanding.” Proceedings of the 2018 EMNLP Workshop BlackboxNLP: Analyzing and Interpreting Neural Networks for NLP, 353–55. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/w18-5446.
Devlin, Jacob, Ming-Wei Chang, Kenton Lee, and Kristina Toutanova. 2019. “BERT: Pre-Training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding.” Proceedings of the 2019 Conference of the North American Chapter of the Association for Computational Linguistics: Human Language Technologies, 4171–86. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/n19-1423.
Merity, Stephen. 2016. The WikiText Long Term Dependency Language Modeling Dataset.
Merity, Stephen, Caiming Xiong, James Bradbury, and Richard Socher. 2016. “Pointer Sentinel Mixture Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1609.07843.

Even a standardized dataset is the first place a benchmark can lose contact with deployment reality. ImageNet’s validation set fixes ResNet-50’s quality target, but its curated, centered photographs are not the frames a camera trap returns at dusk, a gap that section 12.9.2 returns to. A benchmark dataset must be representative enough to expose deployment failures and hard enough to separate models, so that the model architecture and metrics chosen next are tested against meaningful inputs.

12.2.3 Model and metrics

Even on the same ImageNet validation split, two ResNet-50 benchmarks cannot be compared if one team alters layer widths or swaps the runtime while the other changes the accelerator. In systems benchmarking, the model defines the execution workload because its computational graph sets arithmetic intensity, memory footprint, and communication topology. Selecting and fixing the benchmark model determines whether measured performance variations reflect architectural efficiency, software runtime optimizations, or raw accelerator capacity. Evaluating an architectural modification requires holding the runtime and hardware target constant. Conversely, evaluating hardware platforms or software execution engines requires standardizing on a fixed, representative model graph so that throughput differences isolate execution efficiency rather than mathematical changes to the workload.

Baseline models establish the empirical reference point for a target domain. Rather than toy algorithms, systems benchmarks rely on canonical workloads whose computational profiles and memory access patterns are well-characterized. In vision, ResNet-50 plays this role, which is why the chapter’s claim is stated on it. In language processing, BERT5 serves as a standard baseline because its multi-head self-attention and dense feed-forward blocks exercise both compute-bound matrix multiplications and memory-bandwidth-bound reduction operations. However, a baseline specification cannot stop at the mathematical graph. Two implementations of the identical architecture across different frameworks or runtimes often behave differently at runtime. Differences in kernel fusion (such as fusing normalization and activation functions directly into general matrix multiply (GEMM) output epilogues to avoid round-trips to high-bandwidth memory (HBM)), kernel launch overhead, and memory allocator behavior can exceed the performance impact of minor architectural variations. A comparison that isolates hardware must control the runtime implementation alongside the mathematical model. A runtime comparison instead names that implementation as the intervention and documents both stacks.

5 BERT (bidirectional encoder representations from transformers): BERT-Large (340M parameters) is a language-processing workload in MLPerf Inference. The benchmark fixes the task, data, quality target, and applicable execution scenarios so systems can be compared on the same workload. BERT latency still depends on sequence length, batching, and implementation.

A benchmark must select the operational regime relevant to its engineering question. In training, memory capacity and interconnect bandwidth can be binding constraints, because storing forward activations, backward gradients, and optimizer states across multiple accelerators limits batch size and demands high-bandwidth collective communication. Training benchmarks therefore measure time-to-accuracy under fixed convergence criteria rather than raw iteration speed alone. In inference, backward passes and optimizer states disappear. Evaluation instead emphasizes the serving objective, such as tail latency under SLAs, while low-batch autoregressive decode often faces bandwidth pressure. Transitioning a model to production frequently requires precision reduction, quantizing FP32 or BF16 weights and activations to INT8 or FP8. Moving eligible tensors from a 16-bit to an 8-bit format halves their raw storage and can reduce traffic or raise supported arithmetic throughput. Whole-model gains depend on the execution path, and validation must establish whether numerical error stays within the quality target. A development-to-deployment comparison may need both regimes because efficient training does not establish serving feasibility; a deployment decision for a fixed pretrained model can focus on inference.

Evaluation metrics6 translate raw model behavior into numbers that can be compared, ranked, and used to make engineering decisions. The difficulty lies in choosing the right numbers. A metric that captures accuracy but ignores latency may declare the winner to be a model too slow for production; one that rewards throughput but ignores energy may optimize for a deployment budget that does not exist. Table 3 categorizes evaluation measures by the failure mode each exposes and the deployment context it serves.

6 Metric: In mathematics, a metric is a distance function satisfying strict axioms including the triangle inequality. ML borrows the term loosely for quantitative measures such as BLEU and perplexity, which are scoring rules rather than mathematical metrics. Leaderboard rankings can change when the evaluation protocol, dataset slice, or metric weighting changes, making the choice of metric an engineering decision that shapes which system wins, not just how performance is measured.

The metrics in table 3 expose physical trade-offs governed by the accelerator architecture. Throughput measures aggregate processing capacity, whereas latency measures individual request completion time. These two objectives conflict directly at the hardware boundary. Maximizing throughput requires aggregating requests into larger batches to amortize weight-loading memory bandwidth across multiple inputs, raising arithmetic intensity on tensor units. That batching mechanism inflates per-sample latency, because individual requests wait in arrival queues while a batch forms, and larger matrix operations take longer to complete. Furthermore, arithmetic mean latency masks tail behavior. Queuing jitter, memory allocation stalls, and accelerator thermal throttling cause execution times to diverge; a system with a 10 ms mean latency can still suffer a 500 ms p99 latency that violates service-level agreements. Benchmarks must therefore report distribution percentiles (p50, p95, p99) rather than averages. Compound metrics such as samples/second/watt compress multiple dimensions into a single scalar, simplifying high-level comparisons but obscuring where bottlenecks reside. Reporting atomic metrics (latency distributions, throughput, and power draw) alongside compound figures isolates which physical constraint limits the system.

↪ The batch size at which waiting for a batch to form pushes latency past its budget is measured with a batch-size sweep in Formation delay and the latency-throughput frontier.

7 BLEU (bilingual evaluation understudy): Introduced by IBM in 2002, BLEU measures translation quality through modified n-gram precision with a brevity penalty against reference translations (Papineni et al. 2002). BLEU is a canonical example of Goodhart’s Law in ML, because optimizing for n-gram matches can reward surface-level word overlap even when meaning, fluency, or deployment usefulness diverges from the target.

Papineni, Kishore, Salim Roukos, Todd Ward, and Wei-Jing Zhu. 2002. “Bleu: A Method for Automatic Evaluation of Machine Translation.” Proceedings of the 40th Annual Meeting on Association for Computational Linguistics - ACL ’02, 311–18. https://doi.org/10.3115/1073083.1073135.

Task-specific metrics evaluate the algorithmic dimension, quantifying whether the model solves the statistical problem. Classification workloads evaluate accuracy (the fraction of correct predictions), precision, recall, and the F1 score (Sokolova and Lapalme 2009). Precision and recall isolate true positive rates from false alarms, which is essential when class distributions are imbalanced. Continuous estimation tasks rely on Mean Squared Error (MSE) or Mean Absolute Error (MAE), while sequence generation uses specialized scoring rules such as BLEU7 to measure modified n-gram precision against human reference translations (Papineni et al. 2002).

Task metrics evaluate algorithmic quality in isolation, but deployment viability depends on physical machine constraints. Model capacity, measured in parameter count and memory footprint, determines whether weights fit into on-chip SRAM or accelerator memory, or whether they spill across high-latency interconnects. Per-inference execution latency dictates whether a system meets real-time deadlines, while energy consumption per inference (measured in joules or millijoules) bounds thermal dissipation and battery life.

Benchmarks must also account for measurement sensitivity across software runtimes. As established in Model Training, different frameworks handle loss computation, kernel fusion, and gradient accumulation differently, altering reported benchmark numbers. Subtle implementation differences shift measured accuracy and latency enough to invert benchmark rankings when candidate models differ by narrow margins. Examples include whether batch normalization is configured to use stored running statistics or current mini-batch statistics during evaluation, and how reduction kernels reorder non-associative floating-point additions. Controlling how the runtime is driven and how those metrics are timed is the job of the benchmark harness.

12.2.4 Benchmark harness

When host software initiates an inference or training step on an accelerator, the framework runtime enqueues commands onto a work queue (such as a CUDA stream or hardware command buffer) and returns control to the CPU in microseconds. A naive timer wrapping that host-level call measures queue dispatch rather than device execution. Avoiding such measurement traps is the job of the benchmark harness, the software and instrumentation scaffold that feeds inputs to the system under test, coordinates execution across the asynchronous host-accelerator boundary, collects timing and power traces, and enforces reproducibility. A rigorous harness inserts explicit device synchronization barriers (such as torch.cuda.synchronize()) or queries on-device timestamp events (such as torch.cuda.Event), ensuring that recorded timings cover the declared device-work interval rather than host enqueue overhead.

The timer endpoints determine which work enters the result. Figure 1 separates the host call, the synchronized host interval, and the device-event interval for one inference.

These intervals answer different questions. Earlier device work must finish before the host timer starts. A synchronized host interval then measures elapsed completion time under the stated synchronization boundary, whereas events measure the interval between their positions in the accelerator stream. A report must state those boundaries before comparing two implementations; neither method includes work that falls outside them.

Harness architecture must align with the operational regime of the deployment target. In server environments, the harness generates concurrent request traffic using an open-loop load generator, often parameterizing inter-arrival times with a Poisson distribution.8 An open-loop harness issues requests according to an independent arrival schedule regardless of server responsiveness. This decoupling exposes queuing delays and tail latency (such as p99 response times) when arrival bursts saturate available compute.

8 Poisson distribution: Named after Siméon Denis Poisson, who formalized it in 1837 while modeling wrongful conviction rates in French courts. The distribution models independent events at a constant average rate \((\ArrivalRate)\) and is a common baseline for server request arrivals. Real ML serving traffic often violates its assumptions through burstiness, correlation, and time-varying rates. A Poisson harness can therefore misestimate tail latency unless traces or stress cases cover those production patterns.

In contrast, a naive benchmarking client often employs a closed-loop pattern: sending a request, blocking until the response arrives, and only then issuing the next request. This design introduces the severe measurement distortion known as coordinated omission. Consider a server that experiences a transient 500 ms garbage collection pause or memory reallocation stall. In an open-loop production environment, independent users continue submitting queries, causing dozens of requests to accumulate in the server’s ingress queue; once the pause ends, every queued query suffers inflated tail latency. In a closed-loop benchmark, however, the client blocks idly during the entire 500 ms pause, sending zero traffic while the server is struggling. The benchmark records a single slow query and zero queue backlog. By coordinating request generation with server responsiveness, the closed-loop harness artificially paces traffic, systematically erasing the very queue collapse and p99 latency spikes it was constructed to measure.

The missing requests are the source of the distortion. Figure 2 aligns the two arrival policies against one server pause so that the change in offered work remains visible.

A closed-loop result is valid for a population whose clients wait in this way. It does not by itself estimate latency for an independent arrival stream, because it reduces the offered load precisely when the server slows. The harness must therefore reproduce the deployment arrival policy or report why a different policy answers the intended question.

Offline batch processing eliminates arrival scheduling entirely; the harness pre-stages large input batches in memory to saturate accelerator execution units, maximize memory bandwidth utilization, and measure peak sustained throughput.

In embedded and edge deployments, workloads are neither stochastic network streams nor unconstrained batch queues. Instead, inputs arrive sequentially from physical sensors (such as an audio analog-to-digital converter or an image sensor) governed by fixed hardware sampling clocks. The harness must inject these sensor streams through direct memory access into limited on-chip SRAM buffers, verifying that each inference completes within the inter-sample arrival deadline to prevent buffer overflow. Furthermore, edge harnesses must integrate physical instrumentation, such as shunt resistors or external power analyzers, to sample voltage and current across active execution cycles and low-power sleep states, capturing the true energy consumed per inference.

Reproducibility requires specifying which runtime and environmental conditions are controlled and which are part of the workload. Warm-up matters at every scale, from a single kernel to a whole run, because modern accelerators and runtime frameworks have substantial cold-start transients. Initial inference passes trigger on-demand memory allocations, dynamic just-in-time (JIT) compilation, driver initialization, and weight transfers from secondary storage into accelerator memory. For steady-state measurements, the harness executes unmeasured warm-up iterations until caches populate, memory allocators stabilize, and the intended thermal operating state is reached. Controlled experiments may fix clocks and remove competing processes; deployment tests retain and report the relevant DVFS policy and contention. Cold-start measurements time initialization separately instead of discarding it. Finally, telemetry logging must minimize the probe effect by recording execution timestamps and performance counters into non-blocking, pre-allocated memory buffers. The remaining instrumentation overhead should be checked against a minimally instrumented run.

12.2.5 Specifications and run rules

Even a well-instrumented harness produces an uninterpretable number if the report omits the machine and the rules under which the harness ran. A throughput figure reported without accelerator model, memory bandwidth, or operating clocks conceals whether an observed speedup stems from algorithmic efficiency or upgraded silicon, and a result reported without its seed policy conceals whether it is reproducible at all, because the stochastic processes catalogued in section 12.4.2 mean that two runs on identical hardware can produce different numbers. System specifications record the computational environment, the physical hardware and software stack that establishes the Machine (\(\text{M}\)) boundaries of the system, while run rules govern how the benchmark executes on it. Because every execution metric reflects the interaction between an algorithm’s arithmetic intensity and a machine’s peak arithmetic throughput, memory bandwidth, and interconnect latency, a protocol must either fix seeds and data order when isolating system effects or require repeated runs and quality thresholds when stochastic variation is part of the workload. Table 4 lists what a reproducible report records.

Reference suites may distribute containerized environments that encapsulate dependencies and configurations, while experimental logs retain training metrics, checkpoints, and any mid-run adjustments. Together, these records turn a one-time measurement into evidence another team can inspect and, when access permits, reproduce.

12.2.6 Interpreting results

Once the harness and run rules produce a number, such as a 2 ms inference latency or a 10,000 images/s throughput figure, a single metric still rarely reveals whether an optimization will succeed in deployment. Turning raw benchmark numbers into an engineering decision requires checking both the experimental intervention and the execution state. First, state what changes and what remains comparable. ResNet-50 versus MobileNet can be a valid architecture comparison at matched task and quality requirements; FP32 versus INT8 can be a valid precision comparison on a supported execution path. Hold the other relevant conditions fixed or report their changes, including batch size, hardware, runtime, input distribution, and service targets. Second, distinguish steady-state execution from initialization, using the harness’s warm-up protocol (section 12.2.4). A single run conflates cold-start overhead (kernel compilation, page faults, and cache warming) with sustained throughput, while a prolonged stress test may instead capture the thermal throttling examined for edge devices in section 12.6.4. Report cold-start and steady-state results according to the deployment question, and record variation across repeated trials. Whether a measured difference exceeds that variation is a statistical question, answered with the interval and paired-test rules of section 12.8.1.

Systems Perspective 12.3: Interpreting a benchmark claim
A vendor claims “Our system achieves 10,000 inferences/second on ResNet-50.” Taken alone, the number cannot support deployment planning because the conditions that determine its meaning are unstated.

Four unstated conditions determine what the claim means:

  1. Batch size: Large batches amortize weight transfers and maximize compute utilization but inflate queuing delay; batch 1 minimizes latency but leaves execution units underutilized.
  2. Precision: INT8 execution exploits higher-throughput integer pipelines and cuts memory traffic, but requires quantization-range calibration that can compromise numerical fidelity.
  3. Measurement boundary: The measurement window must state whether it isolates pure accelerator kernel execution or includes host-to-device transfers, JPEG decoding, and tensor format conversions.
  4. Accuracy: The claim must state whether the optimized model retains baseline validation accuracy (such as 76.1 percent Top-1 on ImageNet) or trades task quality for raw execution speed.

Example: “10,000 inferences/second on ResNet-50 at batch size 32, INT8 precision, 76 percent Top-1 accuracy, including JPEG decoding, on NVIDIA H100 at 700 W thermal design power (TDP).”

Systems insight: A throughput figure lacking batch size, precision, measurement scope, and accuracy targets is a marketing metric rather than an engineering specification.

A claim whose workload boundary, precision, and excluded costs cannot be reconstructed should be rejected. Marketing phrases compress such omissions into a few words, usually reporting the most favorable operating point without saying so. Table 5 translates common ones into the technical caveats behind each.

Which metric governs viability depends on the deployment. In safety-critical applications such as medical imaging or autonomous driving, a 1 percent accuracy gain justifies substantial computational cost. In interactive search or speech transcription, tail latency within a tight service-level agreement dominates, so a minor accuracy gain is irrelevant if it breaches the latency budget. Systems evaluation must also guard against benchmark overfitting, in which an optimization targets the idiosyncratic computational patterns of a standardized suite at the expense of production workloads. For example, aggressive kernel fusion or operator tiling tuned exclusively for fixed sequence lengths in a benchmark harness can degrade throughput when deployed against dynamic, variable-length user requests. Robust interpretation requires validating performance across diverse input distributions, perturbed batch sizes, and representative operational duty cycles.

Checkpoint 12.1: Decoding vendor benchmark claims

When evaluating hardware or software based on vendor-reported benchmarks, check whether the claim identifies the workload, measurement boundary, and operating conditions.

12.2.7 A complete reference benchmark

MLPerf Tiny’s anomaly-detection benchmark shows every component fixed at once on one small embedded model. The multi-stage pipeline in figure 3 traces this industrial audio benchmark across nine sequential phases. It carries the task from problem definition in the top chevrons through spectrogram extraction, autoencoder training, and INT8 quantization down to embedded deployment on an ARM mbed OS microcontroller. Each stage fixes boundary conditions for the next. The problem definition dictates the acoustic sampling rate and input representation (Data). The dataset properties and task objective determine feasible model architectures (Algorithm). The microcontroller’s hardware limits (SRAM capacity, energy envelope, and real-time latency deadlines) dictate INT8 quantization and memory-efficient kernel compilation (Machine). Here every choice constrains the next, so a benchmark that measures only classification accuracy or only raw inference latency misses cross-stage interactions, where an engineering decision at any stage narrows the feasible design space downstream.

Its problem definition fixes the same three specifications that the ResNet-50 claim needed. The input is single-channel audio streamed at 16 kHz in fixed one-second windows, which sets a rigid ingestion rate and memory buffer requirement before feature extraction begins. The output is a binary “Normal” or “Anomaly” flag, including any thresholding that turns a reconstruction error into that decision. The performance specification couples a quality metric, area under the ROC curve (AUC), with execution limits. These are a per-inference latency that keeps pace with the one-second input windows, sustained throughput matching the arrival rate, and a resident footprint that avoids out-of-memory faults on the microcontroller.

At the dataset selection stage (the second chevron), the benchmark pipeline ingests representative audio waveform samples of normal machine operation alongside anomalous conditions before spectrogram feature extraction. Domain-specific collections cover different audio tasks: ToyADMOS9 (Koizumi et al. 2019) supports controlled anomaly-detection research, while Google Speech Commands (Warden 2018) supports keyword recognition. Simplified datasets like ToyADMOS are valuable for methodological development but may not capture the full complexity of production environments.

9 ToyADMOS: Developed by NTT Communications in 2019 for acoustic anomaly detection, containing audio recordings from toy car, toy conveyor, and related miniature-machine operating sounds (Koizumi et al. 2019). The “toy” prefix is intentional: the controlled environment enables reproducible benchmarking but can create a domain gap when models are moved to noisier industrial environments with different machines, sensors, vibration, and background sound.

Koizumi, Yuma, Shoichiro Saito, Hisashi Uematsu, Noboru Harada, and Keisuke Imoto. 2019. “ToyADMOS: A Dataset of Miniature-Machine Operating Sounds for Anomalous Sound Detection.” 2019 IEEE Workshop on Applications of Signal Processing to Audio and Acoustics (WASPAA), 313–17. https://doi.org/10.1109/waspaa.2019.8937164.
Warden, Pete. 2018. “Speech Commands: A Dataset for Limited-Vocabulary Speech Recognition.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1804.03209, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1804.03209.

This interdependence between statistical quality and hardware constraints is evident in anomaly detection. In industrial monitoring, extreme class imbalance renders raw accuracy deceptive. If anomalies constitute only 0.1 percent of operating samples, a trivial model that constantly predicts normal operation achieves 99.9 percent accuracy while failing to detect any faults. The benchmark must instead report AUC, which scores how well the model ranks anomalous samples above normal ones across every decision threshold, alongside physical resource metrics.

This multi-metric evaluation approach governs the anomaly detection pipeline, which balances four simultaneous constraints. The model has 270K parameters, and its stored representation and runtime buffers must fit the embedded SRAM budget. Processing takes 10.4 ms per inference, which maintains real-time sampling. Detection accuracy is 0.86 AUC. Energy consumption is 516 µJ per inference, within the target battery budget. Evaluating across all four axes prevents declaring a model superior when its accuracy gain stems from an architecture too large to fit in memory or too power-hungry to survive sustained deployment.

The deployed anomaly detection workflow in figure 3 records the four measurements from section 12.2.3 in its terminal reporting table (lower right). For the fully connected autoencoder (FC-AE) on the ARM mbed OS target, each certifies something different. Parameter count and per-inference latency certify computational resources, the AUC certifies task effectiveness, and energy per inference certifies operational efficiency.

On a microcontroller, each metric governs a different failure: energy per inference sets battery life on untethered devices, model size decides whether weights fit in fast on-chip SRAM or spill into off-chip DRAM, and latency determines whether streaming audio is processed without dropped frames. The metrics trade against one another: reducing model size below 270K parameters can reduce memory traffic and execution time on a supported path, but risks degrading the 0.86 AUC detection accuracy. Whether these measurements constitute a passing benchmark depends on the operational envelope; the benchmark specification standardizes the evaluation protocol, but application requirements dictate the acceptance thresholds.

The reference flow fixes the task, data, model, and measurement boundary, but it measures one model on one device at one scope. Before choosing that scope, a benchmark should pass three methodological checks.

Checkpoint 12.2: Benchmarking methodology

Three practices distinguish rigorous benchmarks from misleading ones:

A benchmark that passes those checks has still made a second choice, often implicitly. It decides how much of the system it measures. A single kernel, the whole model, and the request path around the model each answer a different question, and each fails in a different way.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which core benchmark component is responsible for documenting the exact hardware model, CPU core pinning, GPU driver version, CUDA toolkit, compiler flags, and OS kernel version required to ensure experimental reproducibility?

    1. System specifications
    2. Dataset split definition
    3. Evaluation metric formula
    4. Problem definition

  2. When designing a benchmark suite for an edge computer vision model deployed on a battery-powered security camera with passive cooling, which set of evaluation metrics provides the most complete assessment of deployment viability?

    1. Peak offline throughput in FP32 without thermal monitoring
    2. Energy per inference (mJ), active vs. idle power consumption across the device duty cycle, memory footprint (SRAM/DRAM usage), sustained latency under thermal equilibrium, and deployed-model quality on representative and critical input slices
    3. Only the model parameter file size on disk in megabytes
    4. Top-1 validation accuracy measured on an uncompressed server GPU

  3. True or False: When evaluating model compression techniques (such as INT8 quantization or structured pruning), validating that the compressed model achieves a \(4\times\) reduction in file size with \(<0.5\%\) top-1 accuracy loss is sufficient to guarantee proportional speedups and energy savings on any target deployment hardware.

  4. In standardized benchmarking suites, the formal component that defines the mandatory execution constraints, convergence thresholds, warmup requirements, and statistical aggregation procedures to ensure fair cross-platform comparisons is called the ____.

  5. Order the following execution steps of a standardized benchmark harness protocol from beginning to end for a controlled steady-state measurement workflow: (1) Execute the timed measurement loop while collecting high-resolution hardware timestamps, (2) Apply the selected environment controls, such as CPU affinity pinning and any documented clock policy, (3) Compute summary statistics (mean, median, p90, p99, standard deviation) and confidence intervals, (4) Execute unmeasured warm-up iterations to load model weights and warm instruction/data caches, (5) Perform verification check to ensure model output predictions match ground truth quality thresholds

  6. A vendor publishes a marketing claim stating their new AI accelerator achieves ‘\(120\text{ TFLOP/s}\) on Transformer inference.’ Which combination of parameters is essential to make this throughput figure technically actionable and reproducible?

    1. Only the silicon process node (e.g., \(4\text{ nm}\)) and the data center room temperature
    2. Numerical path (e.g., FP32 vs. FP16), batch size, sequence length, software/compiler stack version, and sustained thermal operating state
    3. The brand of server power supply and the serial number of the host CPU
    4. Only the parameter count of the model, without specifying batch size or precision

See Answers →

12.3 Benchmarking Granularity

A kernel that runs 3\(\times\) faster in isolation may deliver no end-to-end speedup if the data pipeline cannot keep the accelerator fed. In an illustrative request, a 3× inference speedup applied to a 10 ms model stage inside a 50 ms request yields only about a 1.2× end-to-end improvement, or less if the optimization increases memory pressure. The dilution makes the measurement boundary a design choice. Carpentry specifies how a measurement is built, while benchmarking granularity specifies what physical boundary it measures. System validation spans multiple architectural scales, from individual tensor operations to complete production pipelines, with each granularity diagnosing distinct bottlenecks:

  • Micro benchmarks isolate individual components: kernel execution time, memory bandwidth utilization, and single-layer numerical behavior. These diagnose where low-level hardware inefficiencies occur.
  • Macro benchmarks evaluate composed model subsystems: full model training convergence, inference pipeline throughput, and dataset accuracy metrics. These reveal what architectural trade-offs exist across layer compositions.
  • End-to-end benchmarks measure complete operational pipelines: client request-to-response latency including preprocessing, training time-to-accuracy including storage I/O, and model robustness on production data distributions. These show whether the combined system satisfies real-world service-level objectives.

Component speedup rarely survives as end-to-end benchmark speedup.

Optimization techniques operate across distinct granularities: kernel fusion targets micro-level execution, structured pruning alters macro-level parameter structures, and data curation determines end-to-end generalization. System validation must align with these respective boundaries. An isolated micro-benchmark may show substantial kernel speedup, yet a macro-benchmark can reveal that increased activation memory footprints force smaller batch sizes that degrade overall throughput. Similarly, an end-to-end benchmark exposes storage or host deserialization stalls that remain invisible at the accelerator kernel level.

Measurement perimeters widen from left to right in figure 4, and the figure inserts one tier between macro and end-to-end. Micro-benchmarks isolate kernel layers (ML Layers) and macro-benchmarks evaluate composed models (ML Model). An application benchmark then adds the supporting host compute around the model (AI Task), and the end-to-end benchmark adds the non-AI compute nodes the request also traverses. The three granularities in this section fold the application tier into end-to-end, but the four-tier view shows where bottlenecks migrate as the boundary widens: out of the accelerator and into the host systems around it.

12.3.1 Micro benchmarks

While end-to-end metrics govern service-level agreements, optimizing an accelerator pipeline requires isolating the specific mathematical operations that consume execution time and energy. Micro-benchmarks serve this diagnostic role by evaluating individual tensor operations in isolation, measuring the accelerator’s hardware primitives directly. When profiling a slow inference service, macro-benchmarks report aggregate latency violations, but only micro-benchmarks reveal whether execution time is dominated by matrix multiplications, self-attention projections, memory copies across the host-accelerator interconnect, or elementwise activation kernels.

↩︎ The compute primitives that micro-benchmarks isolate, vector and matrix operations and special function units, are established in Compute Primitives.

10 cuDNN (CUDA deep neural network library): Released by NVIDIA in 2014, cuDNN provides hand-tuned kernel implementations for convolutions, pooling, and normalization. The benchmarking implication: reported inference latencies depend heavily on which cuDNN version and algorithm autotuner settings were used, making cuDNN version a mandatory element of any reproducible benchmark specification.

Chetlur, Sharan, Cliff Woolley, Philippe Vandermersch, Jonathan Cohen, John Tran, Bryan Catanzaro, and Evan Shelhamer. 2014. “cuDNN: Efficient Primitives for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1410.0759.
Baidu Research. 2016. DeepBench: Benchmarking Deep Learning Operations on Different Hardware.

Tensor operations constitute the primary target of micro-benchmarking because matrix multiplications and convolutions dominate the arithmetic budget of deep neural networks. Highly optimized runtime libraries such as cuDNN10 (Chetlur et al. 2014) provide hand-tuned kernel implementations tailored to specific microarchitectures. Micro-benchmarks also evaluate standalone activation functions (such as ReLU, Sigmoid, and GELU) and structural submodules (such as multi-head attention projections or transformer feed-forward blocks) across standardized input shapes. Benchmark suites such as Baidu’s DeepBench (Baidu Research 2016) formalized this methodology by evaluating raw GEMM, convolution, and collective communication primitives across diverse hardware platforms, isolating silicon-level execution efficiency from higher-level framework dispatch overhead.

Measuring isolated kernels requires rigorous experimental control. Without disciplined measurement protocols, dynamic hardware frequency scaling, asynchronous execution queues, and cache state introduce transient artifacts that distort kernel runtime.

Systems Perspective 12.4: Micro-benchmarking rules

Five rules keep a micro-benchmark measuring the kernel rather than a hardware artifact:

  1. The warm-up rule: Do not measure cold-start iterations as steady-state performance. Modern hardware uses DVFS (dynamic voltage and frequency scaling) and dynamic frequency boosting; caches, kernels, and clocks need warm-up before the measured loop represents sustained behavior.
  2. The variance rule: Report the coefficient of variation (CV) \((\text{CV} = \RepeatedRunStandardDeviation/ \RepeatedRunMean)\), where \(\RepeatedRunStandardDeviation\) and \(\RepeatedRunMean\) are the standard deviation and mean across repeated runs. Investigate values above the protocol’s workload-specific tolerance; common causes include background OS jitter, thermal throttling, and memory contention, and 5 percent is not a universal cutoff.
  3. The “speed of light” (SOL) check: Compare the achieved throughput against the roofline. If a kernel achieves 10 TFLOP/s on an H100 (peak ~989 TFLOP/s FP16, or ~1,979 TFLOP/s FP8 dense), the diagnostic step is to identify the cause of low utilization (often kernel launch latency from too many small kernels) before optimizing the code itself.
  4. The cache-state rule: Set cache state to match the claim. Flush or exceed the L2 cache when measuring cold dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) bandwidth; preserve a warmed cache when measuring steady-state reuse. Otherwise a nominal DRAM result may instead reflect cache bandwidth (~5 TB/s–10 TB/s) rather than DRAM bandwidth (~1 TB/s–2 TB/s).
  5. The synchronization barrier rule: Accelerator kernel launches are asynchronous on the host CPU. Timing GPU operations using CPU clocks without an explicit device barrier (such as torch.cuda.synchronize()) measures host enqueue latency rather than kernel execution; always place synchronization barriers immediately before and after the timed region, or use GPU-side timestamp events (cudaEventRecord).

The roofline model11 of Performance Bounds, which plots the bound of the arithmetic intensity law (principle 6), is a prediction, and a benchmark is the measurement that tests it. The roofline bounds where a kernel can sit; a benchmark determines where it does sit, and the gap between the two is the diagnostic. A kernel far below its predicted ceiling is losing time to something the model omits (launch overhead, poor coalescing, synchronization), which is exactly what profiling looks for. Making the prediction requires the workload’s arithmetic intensity at a named memory interface and the target’s matching compute and bandwidth limits. For an NVIDIA H100 with 989 TFLOP/s of dense FP16 Tensor Core performance (FP32 is 67 TFLOP/s)12 and 3.35 TB/s of memory bandwidth, the ridge point (peak compute divided by peak bandwidth, the intensity at which the two ceilings meet) falls at 295 FLOP/byte. Below it, bandwidth sets the lower ceiling; above it, compute does (Hardware ridge points derives the threshold).

11 Roofline model: Williams et al. (2009) introduced the Berkeley model, named for the visual shape of its performance ceiling. Its ridge point (peak FLOP/s divided by peak bandwidth) separates bandwidth-limited from compute-limited ceilings, suggesting which interventions to test against measured execution.

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

12 FLOP/s (floating-point operations per second): The gap between advertised peak FLOP/s and achieved FLOP/s is the central tension in hardware benchmarking. The H100 advertises 989 TFLOP/s of dense FP16 Tensor Core arithmetic, but real workloads achieve different fractions of peak depending on arithmetic intensity, memory access patterns, precision, and runtime overhead. Reporting peak FLOP/s without utilization context is the most common benchmarking distortion.

↪ The H100 ridge point, with a compute-bound matrix multiplication and a memory-bound element-wise kernel placed on either side of it, is collected for reference in A concrete example: The H100 analysis.

A profiler translates these measurement rules into physical diagnostics by decomposing kernel execution time into the three governing terms of the iron law: data movement, compute throughput, and latency overhead. Comparing the measured utilization of each resource against hardware limits reveals which term dominates the trace.

Systems Perspective 12.5: Measuring the iron law terms

Moving from theory to trace means mapping the iron law equation onto a profiler timeline (like Nsight Systems or PyTorch Profiler).

Measuring the data term \(\left(\frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth}\right)\)

  • Signal: Look for the “Memory Throughput” or “DRAM Bandwidth” line.
  • Formula: \(\AchievedKernelBandwidth = \frac{\DataVolume}{\KernelExecutionTime}\).
  • Diagnosis: If \(\AchievedKernelBandwidth \approx \PeakMemoryBandwidth\) (for example, close to the H100’s 3.35 TB/s peak), bandwidth pressure is a strong candidate bottleneck. Test whether reducing traffic or increasing bandwidth improves time; a high bandwidth reading alone does not establish that changing compute (\(\Operations\)) has no effect.

Measuring achieved compute throughput \((\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw)\)

  • Signal: Distinguish “SM Active,” the fraction of time the GPU’s Streaming Multiprocessors (SMs), its parallel execution cores, have work resident, from executed-operation throughput. A busy multiprocessor does not imply high useful-model FLOP/s.
  • Formula: \(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw = \frac{\Operations}{\KernelExecutionTime}\), reported in TFLOP/s.
  • Diagnosis: If \(\EfficiencyHw \ll 1\) AND \(\AchievedKernelBandwidth \ll \PeakMemoryBandwidth\), neither quoted peak is being approached. Kernels too small to amortize launch latency or too few threads to occupy the SMs are candidate explanations, but low aggregate rates alone do not exclude dependency stalls or an inefficient memory-access path. Separate inter-kernel gaps from in-kernel stalls, then inspect the corresponding dispatch, dependency, occupancy, or memory-access evidence before selecting an optimization.

Measuring the latency term \((\Latency)\)

  • Signal: Look for gaps (empty space) between colored kernel bars on the timeline.
  • Formula: \(\text{Kernel gap fraction} = \frac{\InterKernelGapTime}{\KernelExecutionTime+ \InterKernelGapTime}\).
  • Diagnosis: A “Sawtooth” pattern (Compute, Gap, Compute, Gap) suggests exposed waiting; correlate CPU dispatch, dependencies, and transfers before attributing it to software overhead. If dispatch dominates, candidates include kernel fusion, covered in Kernel fusion on the accelerator, or the CUDA Graphs of Graph capture and JIT compilation. They capture a repeated sequence of GPU launches so the runtime can replay it with less CPU dispatch overhead.

Profilers supply the measurements this mapping needs. A framework profiler such as the PyTorch Profiler isolates the dominant layer on the step timeline. A kernel profiler such as NVIDIA Nsight Compute explains it from on-chip performance counters, the top-down method developed on a training step in Identifying Bottlenecks. Counter collection and kernel replay perturb timing, so diagnostic traces are compared against a minimally instrumented run. The benchmarking use is narrower, confirming or refuting a prediction.

↩︎ The feeding problem, the wall-clock time an accelerator loses waiting on input I/O, is established in The feeding problem.

These granular measurements enable precise kernel optimization, but they cannot reveal how components interact when assembled into complete models. Macro-benchmarks address this gap.

12.3.2 Macro benchmarks

A convolution kernel that hits 80 percent of the roofline in a micro-benchmark can still stall when chained into a 50-layer network. Macro-benchmarks determine whether operator-level gains persist when layers are composed into a complete model. Composing layers introduces physical interactions that isolated benchmarks cannot observe, because intermediate activation tensors expand peak memory footprint, sequential kernel launches expose CPU driver dispatch overhead, and memory access streams from preceding operations evict shared cache lines before downstream kernels can reuse them.

Evaluating composed model architectures and runtime configurations under standardized workloads exposes trade-offs across those interactions, including generalization accuracy on unseen validation data, dynamic memory allocation across batch sizes and context lengths, and aggregate throughput across varied request arrival patterns. An architecture that achieves higher accuracy may require an activation footprint that forces a smaller batch size under device memory constraints, collapsing the computational throughput that justified its adoption. ResNet-50’s training step tests the roofline’s prediction at exactly this granularity, because the prediction is made layer by layer and the measurement is made on the composed model.

Napkin Math 12.1: ResNet-50 on the roofline: Predicted versus measured
Problem: The roofline predicts the fastest an H100 can train ResNet-50, and MLPerf measures how fast it does. How large is the gap, and what does it say about where the time goes?

Prediction: One training image costs about 24.6 GFLOP, three times the 8.2 GFLOP forward pass, because the backward pass computes gradients with respect to both activations and weights. At training batch sizes, the convolution layers reuse each weight across hundreds of images and sit above the 295 FLOP/byte ridge, so the roofline places the step on the compute roof. Dividing the 989 TFLOP/s peak by the work per image gives a ceiling of about 40,200 images/s.

Measurement: In MLPerf Training v3.1, NVIDIA’s 64-accelerator H100 system trained ResNet-50 at about 5,000 images/s per accelerator, which delivers about 123 TFLOP/s of model arithmetic, 12.4 percent of peak.

Diagnosis: The measurement sits 8.0× below the prediction. The roofline did not err; it bounded the convolutions and omitted the rest of the step. Normalization and elementwise layers have intensities far below the ridge and run on the bandwidth slope, the host must decode and augment thousands of JPEG images every second, and every step pays launch and synchronization overhead. The prediction cannot say which of these dominates. A profiler attributes the gap among them (perspective 12.5), and Identifying Bottlenecks works that attribution for a training step.

Systems insight: A prediction and a measurement together locate a bottleneck; either alone does not. The roofline says ResNet-50 could train 8.0× faster on this accelerator, and the measurement says something other than arithmetic is setting its pace.

The claim the chapter inherited, that ResNet-50 runs faster at INT8 than at FP32, needs a different macro-benchmark from this training one. An inference run under MLPerf Inference rules settles it directly, because the ResNet-50 Offline scenario on ImageNet (Deng et al. 2024), run at INT8 and at the higher-precision baseline on the same accelerator across fixed batch sizes with each required to hold the reference top-1 accuracy within the benchmark’s quality gate, reports both throughputs and latencies under one set of rules. Natural language macro-benchmarks apply the same model-level discipline to sequence-to-sequence translation and autoregressive generation, pairing output quality with token generation rate across defined context lengths.

Deng, Jia, Wei Dong, Richard Socher, Li-Jia Li, Kai Li, and Li Fei-Fei. 2024. ImageNet.
Ignatov, Andrey, and Radu Timofte. 2024. AI Benchmark.

Industry-standard suites make these model-level comparisons reproducible across platforms. The MLPerf suite spans data centers, edge appliances, mobile devices, and microcontrollers (section 12.6.3), while EEMBC’s MLMark quantifies inference throughput and energy under strict embedded memory budgets and AI-Benchmark (Ignatov and Timofte 2024) evaluates mobile systems-on-chip across heterogeneous compute units. The chapter’s own deployment measurement moves to MobileNetV2 on the camera-trap board, where even a composed-model benchmark is not yet enough because timing only the model omits the host preprocessing, storage, and transfer stages that surround it in an end-to-end pipeline.

12.3.3 End-to-end benchmarks

In a computer vision service, decoding a compressed JPEG image, resizing, cropping, and normalizing colors on the host CPU can take several times longer than running the model itself on a modern accelerator, leaving high-throughput tensor cores idle while they wait for host memory transfers. Macro-benchmarks that start at a pre-staged tensor never see that stall. End-to-end benchmarks encompass the entire production pipeline, capturing every stage required to ingest raw data, generate model predictions, and deliver results to downstream clients. Across workloads, the primary bottleneck frequently sits in these surrounding stages, where natural language pipelines can be paced by host-CPU subword tokenization and dynamic sequence padding, while object detection pipelines can stall on sequential postprocessing such as non-maximum suppression (NMS) and bounding-box coordinate transformations when those steps remain outside the accelerated graph.

Distributed infrastructure components introduce further performance constraints beyond the compute fabric. Remote storage systems bound training throughput when reading large multimodal datasets, and network interface card (NIC) saturation in distributed clusters throttles collective communication. End-to-end benchmarks must measure pipeline throughput under realistic network latencies, concurrent query arrival patterns, and actual storage I/O constraints to ensure reproducible evaluation of the deployment as an integrated system.

Public benchmark suites rarely measure storage, networking, and compute within a single unified protocol. While MLPerf Training and Inference evaluate end-to-end scenarios more closely than isolated operator tests, their closed divisions deliberately standardize input data loading to isolate accelerator performance from storage and network variability. Consequently, production end-to-end benchmarking remains primarily an internal engineering practice, evaluated through distributed tracing and production telemetry on live query distributions, where the first diagnostic step is separating model execution time from the rest of the request.

12.3.4 End-to-end vs. component latency
