When Clipper’s designers profiled serving pipelines for lightweight models, CPU-side JSON deserialization and interprocess data copying took more wall-clock time than the accelerator’s model execution (Crankshaw et al. 2017). Reporting model latency alone would have hidden the largest cost in the request path, a serialization bottleneck that The serialization bottleneck develops for serving systems. Measured at the request, the critical distinction is therefore between component latency (time spent in model computation) and end-to-end latency (total time from request arrival to response delivery). Because serialization, network hops, and queue wait time can dominate total request time, benchmarks that report only model inference time obscure what the user experiences and overstate the return on model-only optimizations.

Crankshaw, Daniel, Xin Wang, Guilio Zhou, Michael J. Franklin, Joseph E. Gonzalez, and Ion Stoica. 2017. “Clipper: A Low-Latency Online Prediction Serving System.” 14th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 17), 613–27.

Table 6 gives an illustrative latency breakdown for an inference request. The model inference stage that vendors report as their “benchmark” number spans 5 to 100 ms, yet the queue wait time it sits behind ranges from 0 to over 1,000 ms. Under load, the single component a benchmark measures is dwarfed by one it never sees, so the reported number can be a small slice of what the user actually experiences.

These component-level contributions explain why optimizing any single stage yields diminishing returns on end-to-end performance, an optimization ceiling formalized by Amdahl’s Law (principle 8). The bound itself appears as equation 11.1, where hardware acceleration states it for an accelerated path; the notebook below applies it to a measured pipeline.

Napkin Math 12.2: Amdahl's Law: Optimization ceiling
Problem: A vision pipeline spends 8 ms on preprocessing (JPEG decode, resize, normalize) and 10 ms on inference. If inference alone is optimized by 5×, how much does end-to-end latency actually improve?

Math: Assume fixed work, sequential stages, and unchanged preprocessing, with no queueing feedback. Optimizing inference from 10 ms to 2 ms reduces total latency from 18 ms to only 10 ms, a 1.8× improvement rather than 5×. Amdahl’s Law formalizes this ceiling: if inference, the part being accelerated, is fraction \(\ParallelFraction\) of total latency, then even infinitely fast inference yields at most \(1/(1-\ParallelFraction)\) speedup, because the preprocessing fraction \(1-\ParallelFraction\) remains. With preprocessing at 44.4 percent of total latency, the remaining fraction \(1-\ParallelFraction\) is 0.444, which yields a maximum achievable speedup \(1/(1-\ParallelFraction)\) of 2.25×, regardless of model optimization.

Systems insight: Aggressive model optimization yields disappointing end-to-end results whenever the nonmodel fraction dominates. Any component speedup quoted in isolation shrinks once the untouched stages are measured alongside it, and the shortfall grows as those stages take a larger share of the pipeline. Comprehensive benchmarks must either include preprocessing in measurements or state explicitly that reported speedups apply only to the inference component.

A latency report that supports a deployment decision therefore states which components it includes, the load it was measured under, and whether each figure is a component or an end-to-end number. Choosing that boundary is the trade-off between isolating a component bottleneck and measuring what the user experiences.

12.3.5 Granularity trade-offs and selection criteria

Choosing a measurement boundary means deciding which question the benchmark must answer, because an engineer tuning a convolution kernel needs micro-level isolation free of network jitter, an architect comparing ResNet-50 and MobileNetV2 needs macro-level model composition, and a release engineer certifying an SLO needs the full end-to-end request path. Table 7 contrasts the diagnostic focus, scope, and trade-offs across the three granularities.

Selecting a single granularity level is rarely sufficient because benchmarking presents an inherent trade-off between diagnostic isolation and production fidelity. Along the descending diagonal in figure 5, evaluation regimes trade diagnostic power for operational realism. Micro-benchmarks (the red point at the top left) isolate kernel-level memory bandwidth and warp occupancy at the expense of end-to-end context. Macro-benchmarks (the blue center point) capture composed model topologies. End-to-end benchmarks (the green point at the bottom right) encompass live production traffic but obscure the root causes of latency spikes. Effective evaluation requires strategically combining all three tiers to verify that kernel speedups survive full-system deployment.

This trade-off maps directly onto the D·A·M taxonomy. Micro-benchmarks assess the physical capability of the Machine (\(\text{M}\)) executing a primitive Algorithm (\(\text{A}\)). Macro-benchmarks evaluate how the Algorithm (\(\text{A}\)) orchestrates tensor data flow across composed layers on the Machine (\(\text{M}\)). End-to-end benchmarks evaluate how raw Data (\(\text{D}\)) ingestion from external storage and network infrastructure interacts with both Algorithm and Machine under production load. Systematic performance engineering requires evaluating all three tiers to ensure local kernel optimizations translate into global system speedups.

Carpentry and granularity let one team build a trustworthy measurement. Comparing that measurement with another team’s, or with a vendor’s, requires that both were built the same way, and that agreement is what standardized benchmark suites provide.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An e-commerce search service reports that production query latency has increased by \(40\%\), violating its SLA. Which benchmarking workflow represents the most effective top-down diagnostic strategy?

    1. Immediately rewrite all GEMM kernels in CUDA assembly without measuring the higher layers
    2. Run isolated microbenchmarks on the GPU memory bus to determine peak DRAM bandwidth
    3. Start with an end-to-end pipeline benchmark to isolate latency contributions across database lookup, tokenization, model inference, and reranking; next run macrobenchmarks on the slowest stage; then use microbenchmarks and kernel profilers to optimize the specific bottleneck operator
    4. Benchmark only the isolated tokenization library on CPU and assume the rest of the pipeline is unaffected

  2. Consider the following three benchmarking tasks:

  1. Timing a single \(4096 \times 4096\) FP16 matrix multiplication in cuBLAS.
  2. Measuring the forward-pass execution time of a complete ResNet-50 model on a single GPU.
  3. Measuring total latency for an image upload, server decompression, feature extraction, neural network classification, and database metadata write. How are tasks (I), (II), and (III) classified by benchmarking granularity?
    1. End-to-end, (II) Micro, (III) Macro
    1. Macro, (II) Micro, (III) End-to-end
    1. Micro, (II) End-to-end, (III) Macro
    1. Microbenchmark, (II) Macrobenchmark (model-level), (III) End-to-end system benchmark

  1. Compare microbenchmarks, macrobenchmarks, and end-to-end benchmarks along the axes of diagnostic isolation and real-world representativeness.

  2. True or False: If an optimized FlashAttention kernel achieves a \(4\times\) microbenchmark speedup over a standard attention implementation, the complete language model inference service hosting that model is mathematically guaranteed to run \(4\times\) faster end-to-end.

  3. In roofline analysis, an accelerator has a peak compute performance of \(989\text{ TFLOP/s}\) (dense FP16) and a memory bandwidth of \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\), yielding a machine ridge point of \(\RooflineRidgePoint \approx 295\text{ FLOP/byte}\). When serving a Transformer model with batch size \(b=1\), the arithmetic intensity is only \(I = 4.8\text{ FLOP/byte}\). What is the roofline upper bound on achieved throughput as a fraction of peak compute throughput for this workload?

    1. Approximately \(1.6\%\) of peak compute throughput (memory-bandwidth bound)
    2. Exactly \(100\%\) because modern tensor cores execute batch \(b=1\) at peak speed
    3. Approximately \(50\%\) due to pipeline bubbles and kernel launches
    4. Approximately \(85\%\) because matrix-vector multiplications are compute-bound

  4. An image classification inference pipeline takes \(18\text{ ms}\) per request in a serial CPU baseline: \(8\text{ ms}\) in image decoding/preprocessing and \(10\text{ ms}\) in neural network execution. If only the model execution is migrated to a specialized NPU that accelerates the neural network by \(5\times\) (reducing inference time from \(10\text{ ms}\) to \(2\text{ ms}\)), with the other \(8\text{ ms}\) unchanged and no added transfer or queueing effects, what is the resulting end-to-end pipeline latency and speedup?

    1. \(2.0\text{ ms}\) latency and \(9.0\times\) speedup
    2. \(3.6\text{ ms}\) latency and \(5.0\times\) speedup
    3. \(10.0\text{ ms}\) latency and \(1.8\times\) speedup
    4. \(16.0\text{ ms}\) latency and \(1.1\times\) speedup

12.4 Benchmark Suites

Without that shared measurement protocol, a team evaluating five candidate system-on-chip (SoC) designs for the camera trap’s next board faces incompatible claims. Vendor A reports 8 TOPS at INT8; Vendor B reports 15 TOPS at INT4; Vendor C reports inference latency on a proprietary model; Vendor D cites MLPerf scores from two generations ago; Vendor E provides only peak throughput at maximum batch size. None of these figures can be directly compared because each vendor isolates a favorable metric under idiosyncratic conditions, obscuring true operating trade-offs. Standardized benchmark suites resolve this fragmentation by enforcing uniform measurement harnesses and evaluation rules across every submission.

Each generation of benchmark suites arose to close a gap of exactly this kind, when the metrics of the day stopped predicting what users experienced. Their history explains the rules modern suites enforce.

12.4.1 From Whetstone to MLPerf

Three shifts mark that history: from isolated kernels to representative application suites, from single-metric throughput to multi-objective energy profiles, and from generic compute metrics to domain-specific execution constraints. The earliest computing benchmarks were open to benchmark gaming. Whetstone (Curnow and Wichmann 1976) used a synthetic mix of scientific operations, while LINPACK (Dongarra et al. 1979) measured dense linear-system solving. Because both relied on predictable computational loops, compiler and hardware vendors could optimize for the benchmark kernels (unrolling synthetic loops, or tailoring cache tiling to exact matrix dimensions) without improving general application performance. SPEC CPU (1989) addressed this by evaluating complete, portable software suites that stressed integer branching, irregular memory hierarchies, and floating-point pipelines (Dixit 1993). The same vulnerability applies to ML, since model-specific kernel tuning can overfit a single workload. Model compression claims therefore require validation on representative tasks such as ResNet-50 and BERT across the full software runtime.

Curnow, H. J., and B. A. Wichmann. 1976. “A Synthetic Benchmark.” The Computer Journal 19 (1): 43–49. https://doi.org/10.1093/comjnl/19.1.43.
Dongarra, J. J., J. R. Bunch, C. B. Moler, and G. W. Stewart. 1979. LINPACK Users’ Guide. SIAM. https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611971811.
Dixit, Kaivalya M. 1993. “Overview of the SPEC Benchmarks.” In The Benchmark Handbook for Database and Transaction Systems, 2nd ed., edited by Jim Gray. Morgan Kaufmann.
Coleman, Cody, Deepak Narayanan, Daniel Kang, Tian Zhao, Jian Zhang, Luigi Nardi, Peter Bailis, Kunle Olukotun, Christopher Ré, and Matei Zaharia. 2019. “Analysis of DAWNBench, a Time-to-Accuracy Machine Learning Performance Benchmark.” ACM SIGOPS Operating Systems Review 53 (1): 14–25. https://doi.org/10.1145/3352020.3352024.

13 MLPerf: Launched in 2018 by a consortium of industry and academic institutions, MLPerf takes its name from “ML” combined with “Perf” (performance), echoing SPEC’s benchmarking tradition. MLPerf’s design principles (representative workloads, full-system measurement, and open submission) directly address the gaming that plagued Whetstone and LINPACK. Vendors who could previously report peak kernel throughput on cherry-picked problem sizes must now report end-to-end system performance on standardized tasks (Mattson et al. 2020; Reddi et al. 2019).

Mattson, Peter, Christine Cheng, Cody Coleman, Greg Diamos, Paulius Micikevicius, David Patterson, Hanlin Tang, et al. 2020. “MLPerf Training Benchmark.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 2: 336–49.

Distributed computing added a further lesson, that component-level microbenchmarks fail to predict cluster-level throughput when interconnect latency, serialization, and collective communication dominate wall-clock time, as they do in distributed ML training. To capture these interactions, DAWNBench (Coleman et al. 2019) pioneered time-to-accuracy evaluation, tying hardware throughput to algorithmic convergence. Maximizing samples per second has no engineering value if numerical instability or poor batch sizing prevents the model from reaching its target validation accuracy. MLPerf13 (2018) combined these lessons and institutionalized full-system measurement, multi-objective constraints, and representative workloads spanning vision, language, and recommendation (Mattson et al. 2020; Reddi et al. 2019).

Energy followed the same path. SPEC Power (2007) (Lange 2009) and Green500 (2007) (Feng and Cameron 2007) made performance per watt a ranking metric for servers and supercomputers. MLPerf Power (MLCommons 2024b) carried it to ML workloads, whose rapid power swings and shared host components demand calibrated instruments and explicit measurement boundaries (section 12.7.4).

Feng, Wu-chun, and Kirk W. Cameron. 2007. “The Green500 List: Encouraging Sustainable Supercomputing.” Computer 40 (12): 50–55. https://doi.org/10.1109/MC.2007.445.
MLCommons. 2024b. MLPerf Power Measurement.
Howard, Andrew G., Menglong Zhu, Bo Chen, Dmitry Kalenichenko, Weijun Wang, Tobias Weyand, Marco Andreetto, and Hartwig Adam. 2017. “MobileNets: Efficient Convolutional Neural Networks for Mobile Vision Applications.” CoRR abs/1704.04861.

Energy benchmarking also exposes algorithmic efficiency. Compression and efficient architectures such as MobileNet (Howard et al. 2017; Sandler et al. 2018) save energy by reducing operations and off-chip traffic. Traffic reduction is a primary lever, because a DRAM read costs orders of magnitude more energy than an arithmetic operation (Where precision saves energy).

As specialized accelerators emerged, generic benchmarks failed to capture domain-specific execution constraints. Data center training clusters optimize aggregate throughput within rack-level power envelopes, mobile AI operates within thermal limits (typically under 5 watts), and embedded microcontrollers function within milliwatt budgets. These budgets decide whether a benchmark prioritizes samples per second or joules per inference. Application requirements add functional constraints such as calibration for clinical models, hard tail-latency cutoffs for trading systems, and bounded worst-case latency for autonomous vehicles.

Because ML workloads couple tensor execution units, memory bandwidth, and communication fabrics, no single metric or workload can characterize a system. MLPerf therefore splits into distinct deployment suites. MLPerf Training evaluates multi-node scaling and high-bandwidth interconnects under time-to-quality targets (Mattson et al. 2020). MLPerf Inference measures throughput and latency percentiles under SLAs across server and client systems (Reddi et al. 2019). MLPerf Tiny tests ultra-low-power microcontrollers operating with tight memory constraints (Banbury et al. 2021). The cross-cutting MLPerf Power track measures useful work per watt across each environment. Reading the constraint column of table 8 from top to bottom, cluster-scale interconnect bandwidth gives way to latency percentiles at the edge, and finally to strict memory footprints on microcontrollers.

Table 9 summarizes the progression from early synthetic loops to multi-dimensional ML systems evaluation; each row’s lesson survives as a rule that modern suites enforce. Those rules become even more demanding in machine learning, where training and inference add variability that deterministic software benchmarks never faced.

12.4.2 ML measurement challenges

Three lessons from benchmark history (representative workloads, multi-objective evaluation, and integrated system measurement) converge on a requirement unique to machine learning: managing statistical approximation. Traditional computing benchmarks specify correctness checks, which may require exact outputs or permit stated numerical tolerances. In ML systems, low-level engineering choices routinely alter numerical output: aggressive weight quantization, kernel pruning, or reduced-precision accumulators trade accuracy for execution speed and lower memory bandwidth pressure. Consequently, an ML benchmark cannot evaluate execution time in isolation; output accuracy serves as an inviolable operational constraint.

Evaluating an ML system therefore means testing the coupled Data, Algorithm, and Machine (D·A·M) choices together rather than measuring raw compute throughput. A suite earns trust by solving three problems. It must produce valid measurements despite run-to-run noise, finite test sets, unrepresentative workloads, and misaligned metrics. It must then isolate what the hardware-software stack contributes, which is the job of a system benchmark and its roofline diagnostic. Finally, it must make results comparable across organizations, which is the job of community standardization.

ML systems combine several sources of measurement variability that classical software benchmarks were not designed to evaluate together. These fluctuations originate at both the machine and algorithmic layers. On the machine side, sustained execution pushes accelerators against thermal and power envelopes, triggering dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) that throttles processor clocks mid-run. Host operating-system activity (thread scheduling jitter, memory paging, and PCIe bus contention during batch staging) further introduces latency variance across runs. On the algorithmic side, floating-point addition is non-associative, so reduction orders that change across parallel execution threads shift results slightly. Mini-batch shuffling, pseudo-random augmentations, stochastic dropout masks, and weight initialization also perturb the trajectory that stochastic gradient descent takes across a non-convex loss surface.

Distinguishing a genuine systems optimization from background measurement noise requires isolating these overlapping sources of variance with repeated runs, paired comparisons, and confidence intervals. Those statistical controls apply to every benchmark in the chapter, so section 12.8.1 collects them in one place, including how large a test set must be before it can resolve a one-point accuracy difference.

Beyond run-to-run variance, benchmark validity depends on workload representativeness. Synthetic microbenchmarks generate uniform, statically sized tensors directly in accelerator HBM. By executing continuous, compute-bound GEMM operations in an isolated loop, microbenchmarks measure theoretical peak arithmetic throughput. However, they conceal the memory hierarchies and scheduling bottlenecks that dominate production deployments. Synthetic workloads bypass host-to-device PCIe data transfers, input decoding pipelines, dynamic memory allocation churn, and GPU cache evictions. In contrast, trace-based benchmarking replays timestamped request logs recorded from live serving clusters. Production traces capture Poisson and bursty request arrival distributions, variable sequence lengths, and concurrent query streams. Replaying live traces forces the serving infrastructure to manage dynamic batching (grouping requests that arrive within a short time window into one batch), key-value (KV) cache allocation under memory pressure, and queuing delays, stress-testing the system under operational conditions that synthetic steady-state loops obscure.

↩︎ The key-value (KV) cache, the per-request attention state that grows with every generated token, is established in System implications.

A representative workload can still mislead if the evaluation metric fails to reflect deployment constraints. When an engineering team optimizes a single algorithmic figure of merit (such as an offline BLEU score, top-1 classification accuracy, or token perplexity) in isolation from system SLOs, the metric ceases to measure genuine operational fitness. This divergence is the chapter’s second principle, Goodhart’s Law, operating inside a single team’s choice of metric, and once the proxy becomes the sole optimization target it stops reporting overall system health. In neural machine translation, an offline accuracy optimization can break the serving deadline outright.

Example 12.1: Goodhart's Law in action
Scenario: An engineering team optimizes a neural machine translation model for offline BLEU score, increasing the beam search size (the number of candidate partial translations kept at each decoding step) from 1 to 10.

Diagnosis: The larger beam search achieves a 0.5-point BLEU gain on paper but increases candidate evaluations by 10×, increasing inference latency from 50 ms to 200 ms (4× slowdown), exceeding the serving deadline.

Systems lesson: Optimizing offline accuracy without latency constraints invites Goodhart’s Law failures. Production benchmarks must enforce strict SLOs for serving latency (e.g., latency < 100 ms).

The translation example shows an algorithmic change (a tenfold wider beam) multiplying computational load and memory traffic for a marginal quality gain while violating a hardware latency deadline. Fixed benchmarks also assess execution under an unvarying input distribution, which conceals how systems degrade under production distribution shifts. Model and data evaluation must test those distribution limits. System comparisons address a separate question by isolating infrastructure execution efficiency from model architecture and dataset variation. Standardized suites like MLPerf establish reference workloads, datasets, quality targets, and permitted implementation changes, enabling reproducible comparisons of hardware-software execution efficiency within a specified benchmark version and division.

12.4.3 System benchmarks

Hardware specifications advertise peak arithmetic rates that application workloads do not sustain—the gap between an H100’s dense FP16 peak and the model arithmetic ResNet-50 training actually achieves (section 12.1). System benchmarks expose that gap by running standardized ML workloads across hardware architectures, memory systems, and interconnects rather than relying on spec-sheet peaks alone.

The underlying hardware, whether CPUs, GPUs, Tensor Processing Units (TPUs),14 and application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs),15 determines ML-system speed, efficiency, and scale. System benchmarks provide standardized methods for comparing hardware across AI workloads by computational throughput, memory bandwidth, power efficiency, operator coverage, and scaling (Reddi et al. 2019; Mattson et al. 2020).

14 TPU (tensor processing unit): Google’s custom ASIC for neural network workloads. A TPU v4 pod (4,096 chips) delivers 1.1 exaFLOP/s peak BF16 (Jouppi et al. 2023), but benchmarking TPUs requires caution: their systolic-array architecture favors regular tensor operations, so peak FLOP/s overstate performance on irregular workloads like sparse attention or dynamic control flow.

Jouppi, Norm, George Kurian, Sheng Li, Peter Ma, Rahul Nagarajan, Lifeng Nai, Nishant Patil, et al. 2023. “TPU v4: An Optically Reconfigurable Supercomputer for Machine Learning with Hardware Support for Embeddings.” Proceedings of the 50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 1–14. https://doi.org/10.1145/3579371.3589350.

15 ASIC (application-specific integrated circuit): An ASIC’s peak TOPS number applies only to the specific operators it was designed for. An unsupported layer may require a host fallback or a different implementation, adding transfer and coordination costs. This makes operator coverage an early question in any ASIC benchmark: workload compatibility can explain part of the gap between peak and achieved throughput.

System benchmarks serve two functions. For practitioners, they enable informed hardware selection by providing comparative data across configurations. For manufacturers, they quantify generational improvements and guide accelerator development. Both functions require separating what the hardware contributed from what the model and data contributed.

Definition 12.2: Machine learning system benchmarks

Machine learning system benchmarks are standardized evaluation protocols that hold the workload and quality target constant while varying the hardware-software stack, measuring \(\EfficiencyHw = \SustainedComputeRate/ \PeakRate\) (the measured sustained rate \(\SustainedComputeRate\) over the peak rate) and \(\Latency\) to isolate infrastructure efficiency from algorithmic improvements.

  1. Significance: The same ResNet-50 model can report the same ImageNet Top-1 accuracy while delivering very different throughput across hardware stacks, precision formats, batch sizes, and compiler configurations. Only a system benchmark exposes that gap.
  2. Distinction: Unlike algorithmic benchmarks (which vary the Data and Algorithm factors, \(\text{Samples/Task}\) and \(\text{FLOPs/Sample}\), to improve convergence or accuracy), system benchmarks hold the algorithm and quality target fixed and vary the implementation (kernel libraries, quantization formats, batch sizes, and hardware generations). They measure how efficiently the hardware-software stack executes the workload, lowering the iron law’s realized Machine factor (\(\text{Seconds/FLOP}\)) across compute (\(\Operations/(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw)\)), data movement (\(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\)), and fixed latency (\(\Latency\)).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a system benchmark result generalizes across workloads. An accelerator that achieves high utilization on ResNet-50 (a compute-friendly vision workload) may achieve much lower utilization on a recommendation system, whose embedding lookups are bound by memory capacity and bandwidth rather than arithmetic. System benchmarks are workload-specific; no single metric characterizes a hardware platform.

System benchmarks evaluate performance across scales, ranging from single-chip configurations to large distributed systems and covering both training and inference. The ImageNet challenge shows why separating the hardware’s contribution from the model’s is hard to do after the fact. Between 2010 and 2014, top-5 error (the blue line in figure 6) fell from 28.2 percent to 7.3 percent, with the steepest single drop at AlexNet’s arrival in 2012. Over the same years, GPU-accelerated entries (the green bars) rose from zero to over 100 (Krizhevsky et al. 2012; Russakovsky et al. 2015; Gray 2015). These paired series plot contemporaneous empirical trends, not a causal attribution of how much accuracy gain stemmed from hardware throughput versus algorithmic design, training schedules, or dataset expansion. A system benchmark avoids that ambiguity by construction: it fixes the model and quality target so that any change in the result belongs to the hardware-software stack.

While section 12.9.1 evaluates model compression against task accuracy baselines, system benchmarking isolates the execution efficiency of the hardware-software stack under a fixed model. Modern accelerators rarely sustain theoretical peak throughput because physical execution depends on the coupling between tensor shapes and memory hierarchies. ResNet-50 training, which the roofline places on the compute roof, sustains about 12.4 percent of an H100’s peak (notebook 12.1). Parameter-heavy transformer decodes and activation-heavy convolutional passes stress entirely different levels of the memory subsystem, causing achievable throughput to diverge across precision formats.

Measuring sustained utilization rather than peak capability establishes two critical scope boundaries. First, energy consumption scales with execution efficiency, because performance per watt varies widely across hardware platforms, and an underutilized accelerator draws substantial static power for minimal computational output. Second, multi-accelerator execution introduces communication boundaries. While multi-node cluster scaling involves network-topology effects beyond this book’s single-node scope, intra-node benchmarking must account for interconnect bandwidth, memory sharing across accelerators, and gradient synchronization over NVLink or PCIe.

12.4.4 Community-driven standardization

Without shared measurement boundaries, utilization numbers cannot be compared. If one benchmark includes host-side preprocessing and PCIe transfers while another measures only device kernel execution, or if one power meter reads only the accelerator voltage rail while another includes host processors and chassis fans, the resulting figures reflect incompatible boundaries rather than architectural efficiency. No single vendor can resolve this discrepancy, because each has a commercial incentive to draw the boundary around its own hardware strengths. Formal standards bodies move too slowly for hardware and model structures that change every year, so consortia such as MLCommons maintain living benchmark suites. These pair open-source reference implementations with compliance rules so that numbers from different laboratories describe comparable workloads under the same version, division, quality target, and scenario.

Standardizing system benchmarks requires formalizing the D·A·M taxonomy so that evaluations isolate the Machine without confounding changes to the Algorithm or Data. The MLPerf Training benchmark enforces this separation by measuring time-to-train to a specified quality target rather than raw floating-point throughput or epoch duration (Mattson et al. 2020). High TFLOP/s from aggressive low-precision scaling helps only if faster steps outweigh any extra work needed to reach the target, and a run that misses the target is invalid. MLPerf splits submissions into two divisions. In the closed division, every submitter must run the reference model and reach the same quality target, so differences in time-to-train belong to the system. The open division permits model and algorithm changes, so its results compare whole solutions rather than machines. Within the closed division, the rules constrain the workload while permitting specified tunable hyperparameters and approved numerical formats. Quality and compliance checks establish which hardware, compiler, and training-configuration improvements constitute valid submissions.

The MLPerf Inference benchmark applies this discipline to production deployment constraints (Reddi et al. 2019). An accelerator cannot simply report peak throughput under infinite batching if interactive serving requires guaranteed responsiveness. The benchmark therefore fixes how queries arrive, through execution scenarios matched to physical operating regimes: one query at a time for a single interactive user, randomly arriving queries under a tail-latency bound for a shared service, and all inputs supplied upfront for bulk throughput. By standardizing these execution boundaries, community benchmarks account for queueing delays and host-to-device PCIe transfers, making the tested load and timing boundary explicit when interpreting a throughput claim.

MLPerf execution scenarios

The same hardware can report dramatically different benchmark numbers depending on how requests arrive, which explains why vendor claims often fail to predict production performance. Classic MLPerf Inference defines four execution scenarios that characterize distinct traffic patterns, each requiring different optimization strategies (Reddi et al. 2019). Current client and generative-AI benchmark variants also include interactive measurements for latency-sensitive large language model (LLM) workloads, where time to first token and time per output token, the two measured quantities defined in section 12.6.2.2, become central (MLCommons 2026b).

SingleStream

SingleStream processes one request at a time, measuring latency for sequential inference. This scenario models mobile and embedded applications where a single user interacts with the device: a smartphone camera app classifying images, a voice assistant processing speech, or a wearable detecting gestures. The key metric is a tail percentile of per-query latency (the 90th percentile in MLPerf’s rules), and inter-request batching is unavailable because the next query arrives after the previous one completes. Optimization focuses on preprocessing efficiency and power consumption rather than throughput.

MultiStream

MultiStream processes multiple synchronized input streams simultaneously, modeling scenarios like autonomous vehicles with multiple cameras that must be processed together for spatial fusion. The application motivation is synchronized processing, but the benchmark rule is version-specific: current MultiStream rules issue a group of samples per query, wait for its completion before the next query, and report a specified latency percentile. Earlier versions used fixed arrival intervals. Grouped-query latency helps assess multi-input execution; it does not establish a hard-deadline guarantee for a vehicle planning loop.

Server

Server generates requests following a Poisson distribution, simulating cloud API traffic where requests arrive independently and unpredictably. This scenario models web services handling millions of queries from different users. Unlike SingleStream’s guaranteed sequential arrival, Server traffic creates queuing dynamics where multiple requests compete for resources. The key metrics are throughput (queries per second) and the workload’s tail-latency bound, typically set at the 99th percentile, and dynamic batching can improve efficiency by grouping requests that arrive within a time window. Optimization balances throughput against latency SLOs.

Offline

Offline provides all inputs upfront, measuring maximum throughput when latency constraints are removed. This scenario models batch processing pipelines: overnight data processing, scientific computing, or precomputing recommendations. With no per-query latency requirement, systems can tune batch size for sustained throughput within memory and execution limits. The key metric is pure throughput (samples per second), and optimization focuses entirely on hardware efficiency. Table 10 maps the classic execution scenarios, plus the newer Interactive LLM-oriented case, to their deployment contexts and optimization strategies.

Community standards fix the workload, the divisions, and the arrival pattern. The training and inference sections apply those rules to the chapter’s lighthouse workloads, beginning with the one that consumes the most arithmetic per result: training ResNet-50 to its quality target.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why did computing benchmark methodology historically transition away from synthetic instruction-mix microbenchmarks (such as Whetstone and Dhrystone) to representative application suites (such as SPEC CPU)?

    1. Synthetic microbenchmarks required too much memory bandwidth to execute on modern microprocessors
    2. Representative application suites were easier to implement and did not require source code compilation
    3. Synthetic benchmarks lacked realistic memory access patterns and branch behavior, allowing optimizing compilers to artificially game scores via dead-code elimination and loop unrolling
    4. Hardware vendors refused to publish floating-point operations per second for synthetic loops

  2. How do the constraints and primary evaluation metrics differ across the domain-specific variants of the MLPerf benchmark suite?

    1. All MLPerf variants evaluate identical metrics (pure TFLOPS) across different hardware form factors
    2. MLPerf Training focuses on latency SLAs, while MLPerf Inference evaluates multi-node interconnect bandwidth
    3. MLPerf Tiny measures data center power consumption, while MLPerf Power evaluates floating-point peak throughput
    4. MLPerf Training targets time-to-quality on fixed workloads, MLPerf Inference evaluates scenario-defined latency and throughput across server and edge settings, MLPerf Tiny targets embedded memory, latency, and energy constraints on microcontrollers, and MLPerf Power measures useful throughput per watt or useful work per joule within a stated measurement boundary

  3. True or False: The introduction of energy-efficiency benchmarks like SPECpower and Green500 replaced raw throughput benchmarks, because computing systems are now evaluated solely on Joules per operation.

  4. Explain why a single benchmark run is insufficient to characterize ML system performance, identifying at least two distinct hardware or runtime sources of execution variance.
