12.5 Training Benchmarks

Training and inference fail in opposite ways on the same accelerator. A training run stalls when synchronizing gradients across accelerators saturates the interconnect; a serving deployment violates its SLO when request bursts build a queue. The two regimes also measure different resources. Training iteratively refines parameters over billions of samples and must hold parameters, gradients, optimizer state, and the activations retained for the backward pass, a footprint that can far exceed the weights alone (table 1 gives ResNet-50’s). Its figure of merit is wall-clock time to a target accuracy. Inference evaluates fixed parameters against arriving queries, discards the backward graph and optimizer state, and is judged by latency percentiles and throughput under bursty load. A training result and an inference result on the same accelerator therefore measure different resources and cannot stand in for each other, which is why MLPerf maintains separate Training and Inference suites (Mattson et al. 2020; Reddi et al. 2019). This chapter measures training on ResNet-50 and serving on MobileNetV2 (section 12.6).

↩︎ The training memory budget of weights, gradients, optimizer state, and retained activations is established in Reducing optimizer memory.

Because training couples per-step computation with gradient communication and multi-gigabyte state, a team that purchases a larger GPU cluster expecting proportional training-speed gains can discover after procurement that interconnect stalls and memory bottlenecks swallow much of the speedup. Training benchmarks exist to catch that gap before capital is committed. They span six dimensions (table 12), anchored by three, namely convergence metrics that measure learning progress, throughput metrics that measure computational efficiency, and scalability metrics that measure distributed performance.

Definition 12.3: ML training benchmarks

ML training benchmarks are machine learning system benchmarks that measure the time to reach a target quality metric (for example, a specified validation accuracy or loss threshold) on a fixed dataset and model, quantifying the rate of convergence per unit of resource.

  1. Significance: Training benchmarks reveal large gaps invisible to hardware specs. Holding the model and quality target fixed, the time to convergence can vary widely across hardware-software stacks because training performance depends on the full pipeline: data loading \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\), compute utilization \((\EfficiencyHw)\), gradient synchronization \((\Bandwidth, \Latency)\), and fault recovery overhead. A peak FLOP/s spec sheet captures none of these interactions.
  2. Distinction: Unlike inference benchmarks, which measure per-query latency and throughput under load, training benchmarks measure time-to-accuracy across the full optimization loop: data loading, forward pass, backward pass, gradient synchronization, and optimizer step. As scale increases, communication \((\Bandwidth)\) can displace compute \((\PeakRate)\) as the binding constraint; profiling establishes the transition for a particular workload.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that training benchmarks measure “how fast the GPU runs.” At large scale, interconnect bandwidth \((\Bandwidth)\) for gradient synchronization and fault tolerance overhead (checkpoint I/O, straggler mitigation) often dominate the benchmark result more than peak FLOP/s.

Because training represents the largest capital expenditure in many ML organizations, only time-to-accuracy measurement reveals whether added accelerators and faster kernels deliver proportional value rather than dissipating into scaling inefficiencies, input stalls, or communication overhead. After examining how standardized suites track these system-wide gains, this section defines and works the core training metrics (time-to-accuracy, scaling efficiency, utilization, energy, and checkpoint overhead) at the scale of one machine with one to eight accelerators.

12.5.1 Training benchmark motivation

MLPerf Training (Mattson et al. 2020; MLCommons 2024c) provides the standardized framework for this time-to-quality measurement. The longitudinal scaling curves in figure 7 track cumulative relative performance gains across nine model workloads between 2018 and 2024 against the orange Moore’s Law baseline. Every workload curve outpaces cumulative transistor scaling (which reaches 6.6\(\times\) by mid-2024), led by Mask R-CNN climbing to 48.8\(\times\) and ResNet reaching 34\(\times\) as distributed scaling and compiler optimizations compound hardware improvements (Tschand et al. 2024). This trajectory documents improvements in the full hardware-software system beyond transistor scaling alone; the plotted results do not isolate the contribution of competitive pressure from other causes.

The gap between the 6.6\(\times\) transistor baseline and the 34\(\times\) to 48.8\(\times\) workload curves in figure 7 came from exposing and removing bottlenecks that raw silicon TFLOP/s never show, including slow data loading, underutilized accelerators, excessive memory overhead, and communication stalls that erode scaling efficiency. Advertised hardware capability becomes a training speedup only to the extent that an end-to-end training benchmark verifies it under realistic conditions.

Training benchmarks therefore serve four interconnected functions. First, they enable hardware and software optimization by providing vendor-neutral comparisons across accelerator architectures and frameworks (TensorFlow, PyTorch) on standardized tasks, guiding hardware selection for data centers and cloud environments. Software optimizations including mixed-precision training16 and memory-efficient data loading are similarly quantified. Second, they evaluate scalability, because adding GPUs should reduce training time proportionally, yet communication overhead, synchronization latency, and memory bottlenecks limit scaling efficiency in practice. Training benchmarks quantify these losses, revealing whether infrastructure investments deliver proportional returns. Third, they provide cost and energy accountability, because with a single large-scale training run consuming energy on the scale of the GPT-3 estimate in section 12.5.2.4, benchmarks that track cost per training run and power consumption per unit of progress help organizations balance computational power with sustainability goals. Finally, standardized evaluation criteria, controlled randomness, submission rules, and audits make comparisons more reproducible and constrain implementation-specific shortcuts. Serving all four functions requires a matched set of training metrics, anchored by time to quality and backed by diagnostic measures of scaling, utilization, energy, and fault recovery.

16 Mixed-precision training: Uses lower precision for most arithmetic while preserving higher-precision accumulation where needed (Micikevicius et al. 2018). The benchmarking consequence: mixed-precision and full-precision runs are not directly comparable because reduced memory traffic and larger feasible batch sizes can change convergence dynamics. MLPerf addresses this by fixing the accuracy target, making time-to-accuracy the comparable quantity regardless of precision strategy.

Micikevicius, Paulius, Sharan Narang, Jonah Alben, Gregory Diamos, Erich Elsen, David Garcia, Boris Ginsburg, et al. 2018. “Mixed Precision Training.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

12.5.2 Training metrics

No single counter captures all four functions of a training benchmark. A complete evaluation pairs an outcome metric, the wall-clock time required to reach a verified quality target, with diagnostic metrics that explain what paced or interrupted the run, namely sample throughput, multi-accelerator scaling efficiency, compute and memory utilization, total energy, and checkpoint overhead.

Time and throughput

A training cluster that processes twice as many images per second by doubling the minibatch or dropping precision has not sped up training if the run requires twice as many epochs to converge—or never reaches the quality floor at all. That is why the primary metric for evaluating training efficiency is the time required to reach a predefined accuracy threshold. Training time \((\TrainingTime)\) measures how long a model takes to converge to an acceptable performance level, reflecting the overall computational efficiency of the system. Let \(\text{Accuracy}_{\text{val}}(\TimeInstant)\) be the model’s validation accuracy at training time \(\TimeInstant\), and let the target accuracy be the benchmark-specific threshold (for example, 75.9 percent top-1 accuracy for ResNet-50 on ImageNet in MLPerf). Equation 1 formally defines this metric, keeping the benchmark focused on how quickly a system achieves meaningful results: \[\TrainingTime= \inf \big\{\TimeInstant\geq 0 : \text{Accuracy}_{\text{val}}(\TimeInstant) \geq \text{target accuracy} \big\} \tag{1}\]

A run that never reaches the target has no finite time-to-accuracy, preventing raw training speed from rewarding a system that fails the benchmark’s quality criterion.

Throughput,17 often expressed as the number of training samples processed per second, provides an additional measure of system performance. Let \(\ProcessedSampleCount\) be the total number of training samples processed and \(\TrainingTime\) the training time from equation 1. Equation 2 makes the rate explicit by dividing processed samples by training time: \[\text{Throughput} = \frac{\ProcessedSampleCount}{\TrainingTime} \tag{2}\]

17 Throughput: Originating in manufacturing to measure units produced per unit time, the term entered computing in the 1960s batch-processing era. The manufacturing origin carries a systems lesson: throughput and latency are inherently opposed, because batching increases throughput (more units per hour) at the cost of individual item wait time. In ML serving, this manifests as the batch-size trade-off: larger batches improve GPU utilization but increase per-request latency.

MLCommons. 2024c. MLPerf Training Benchmark.

Throughput alone cannot certify system capability. A platform may sustain high sample rates using aggressive low-precision formats or enormous minibatches, yet fail to reach target accuracy because of gradient divergence or poor generalization. In MLPerf Training, for instance, a ResNet-50 benchmark on ImageNet is valid only if the model achieves the required 75.9 percent top-1 validation accuracy (Mattson et al. 2020; MLCommons 2024c). Evaluating throughput strictly as the rate of progress toward convergence ensures that reported gains reflect genuine learning productivity rather than wasted arithmetic cycles.

Scalability and parallelism

Scalability measures how effectively training performance improves as resources are added. Ideally, doubling GPU count should halve training time. In practice, communication overhead, memory bandwidth limits, and parallelization inefficiencies constrain scaling well below linear.

Napkin Math 12.3: Scaling efficiency calculation
Problem: A team trains an image classifier on a fixed dataset. Single-GPU training takes 24 hours. With 8 GPUs, training takes 4 hours. Is this good scaling? Where did the efficiency go?

Step 1: Define scaling efficiency. For strong scaling (fixed problem size, more processors), let \(\Time(1)\) be the training time on a single GPU, and \(\Time(\AcceleratorCount)\) the training time on \(\AcceleratorCount\) GPUs. Equation 3 defines efficiency: \[\text{Eff}_{\text{scaling}}(\AcceleratorCount) = \frac{\Time(1)}{\AcceleratorCount \times \Time(\AcceleratorCount)} \times 100\% \tag{3}\]

Step 2: Calculate efficiency. \(\text{Eff}_{\text{scaling}}(8) = \frac{24\,\text{hours}}{8 \times 4\,\text{hours}} \times 100\%\) = 24/32 = 75 percent

With perfect scaling, 8 GPUs would complete in 3 hours (24 hours/8 GPUs). The actual 4 hours represents 75 percent efficiency.

Step 3: Investigate the efficiency loss. The two elapsed times establish the “missing” 25 percent, but do not attribute it. Table 11 lists illustrative contributions and profiling signals for gradient synchronization, memory copy, load imbalance, and batch-size effects. Strong-scaling comparisons hold total work fixed; time-to-quality comparisons must also account for any change in steps to convergence.

In multi-accelerator data-parallel training (such as 2, 4, or 8 GPUs connected over PCIe or NVLink within a host), each accelerator computes gradients on its own local slice of the minibatch. The AllReduce collective of Data parallelism must sum these gradients so every worker receives the identical global gradient average before the next step. Gradient tensors have one entry per trained parameter, but their byte payload equals weight storage only when their representations match. Moving them across interconnects adds communication whose cost depends on topology, the collective algorithm, and overlap. With fixed total work and ideal partitioning, compute time per step shrinks (\(\IronLawComputeTime \propto 1/\AcceleratorCount\)), while communication may shrink more slowly or grow. Exposed synchronization then occupies a larger fraction of the step; traces determine how much of the observed scaling loss it explains.

Step 4: The systems insight. Scaling efficiency can decrease as \(\AcceleratorCount\) grows when exposed communication and other overheads fail to shrink with per-GPU compute. In this worked example, eight GPUs reach 75 percent efficiency; at larger scales, the same arithmetic makes clear why sophisticated communication and input-pipeline optimization become necessary.

MLPerf reports raw performance, while scaling efficiency can be calculated across matched submissions: a system achieving 2\(\times\) throughput at 50 percent efficiency may be worse than 1.5\(\times\) throughput at 90 percent efficiency, depending on cost constraints.

↩︎ The AllReduce collective that averages gradients across data-parallel workers is established in Data parallelism.

Parallelism strategies (data, model, and pipeline parallelism) and the synchronization each requires are developed in Data parallelism; within one machine of two to eight accelerators, data parallelism is the common case. A benchmark’s job is narrower: to report how much of the added capacity a configuration converts into speedup without degrading convergence. That conversion is scaling efficiency. While strong scaling evaluates whether adding processors reduces training time on a fixed total workload, weak scaling scales the problem size proportionally with processor count to keep the per-device workload constant. It measures whether the system can train larger models or ingest larger datasets within a fixed time budget without communication bottlenecks dominating.

Resource utilization

Training efficiency depends on learning speed, cluster scalability, and how effectively the hardware resources are used. Compute utilization requires an explicit numerator. Model FLOPs utilization (MFU), the metric of definition 8.3, compares the useful model operation rate with the matching hardware peak; activity counters instead report whether units are busy. MFU contrasts with hardware FLOPs utilization (HFU), which counts all executed operations including activation recomputation; recomputation adds executed FLOPs without adding useful model FLOPs for the same step. Comparing them separates useful model work from work expended to relieve memory capacity limits. Low utilization may indicate bottlenecks in data movement, memory access, or inefficient workload scheduling.

For instance, when training ResNet-50 on a dataset too large to fit entirely in host memory, retrieving batches from storage and decoding and augmenting thousands of JPEG images per second on the host can limit throughput even when the accelerators have compute to spare, the crossing from compute-bound to input-bound that Data pipeline traces. If storage retrieval or host preprocessing cannot keep up, the accelerators wait; profiling I/O and compute utilization isolates that stall so prefetching, caching, and parallel input processing can restore sustained throughput.

Memory bandwidth is another limiting factor, as deep learning models require frequent access to large volumes of data during training. If memory bandwidth saturates, increasing compute power alone will not improve training speed. Benchmarks assess how well models use available memory, ensuring that data transfer rates between storage, main memory, and processing units do not become performance bottlenecks.

Energy efficiency and cost

Training GPT-3 was estimated to consume 1,287 MWh of electricity (Patterson et al. 2021). At that scale, energy efficiency metrics quantify the power usage of training workloads so teams can identify systems that reach target accuracy without wasted energy. Fewer iterations reduce energy only when their total energy cost is lower; a more expensive step can offset the reduced count, so suites such as MLPerf Training measure total energy over the complete run to the same quality target. The same power accounting governs inference, where precision is one potential energy lever; section 12.7 works through why INT8 quantization cuts per-inference energy by attacking both memory traffic and arithmetic cost.

Cost considerations extend beyond electricity usage to include hardware expenses, cloud computing costs, and infrastructure maintenance. Training benchmarks provide insights into the cost-effectiveness of different hardware and software configurations by measuring training time in relation to resource expenditure. Organizations can use these benchmarks to balance performance and budget constraints when selecting training infrastructure.

Fault tolerance and robustness

Training workloads often run for extended periods, sometimes spanning days or weeks, making fault tolerance an essential consideration. A resilient system must handle unexpected failures (hardware malfunctions, network disruptions, and memory errors) without compromising accuracy convergence.

Production systems checkpoint for fault tolerance, periodically saving progress so a failure does not restart the run. On a single host, a benchmark records the save interval, the state written, and the pause each save exposes to training. Model weights and optimizer states consume storage bandwidth, and any pause extends wall-clock time to the quality target. Asynchronous saving can overlap I/O with computation, but its staging buffers and competing storage traffic remain part of the measured workload. Cluster recovery and parallel file systems extend this problem beyond the local machine; the local benchmarking lesson is to include the intended saving behavior in sustained performance measurements. MLPerf Training primarily measures time-to-quality under standardized workloads and does not benchmark failure recovery directly.

Training runs are also where reproducibility is hardest, because stochastic processes interact with hardware-specific behavior over many hours. What a training report must preserve to make its result reproducible, the full run envelope rather than the random seed alone, is collected with the other statistical controls in section 12.8.2.

12.5.3 Training performance evaluation

A single time-to-accuracy score tells an engineering team whether a run met its deadline, but not why an eight-GPU configuration lost a quarter of its ideal speedup or whether buying faster interconnects would fix it. Each metric category in table 12 isolates a different way hardware investment can fail to become convergence, turning a headline runtime into an actionable diagnosis.

The scaling notebook supplies a concrete interpretation of table 12. The measured completion times establish a speedup and a resource-efficiency cost, but they do not identify which overhead caused the difference. A procurement decision therefore uses the time-to-quality result first, then the utilization and communication measurements to decide whether additional hardware or a different execution configuration would address the limiting resource.

Across matched runs, GPU boost-clock18 behavior and thermal state must follow the same protocol; otherwise a short fast run can be mistaken for a durable improvement. The repeated-run controls in section 12.8.1 determine whether the difference exceeds experimental uncertainty.

18 GPU boost clock: Dynamic frequency scaling raises clocks above base when thermal and power headroom permit. Short benchmark runs can capture this burst performance, while sustained training may settle to a different operating point. The benchmark must document the measured thermal state and clock policy.

In the worked case, 8 GPUs reach 75 percent scaling efficiency. If the required quality target and completion deadline are met, the configuration clears the time constraint, but the remaining scaling overhead still matters for cost. If the deadline is missed, the efficiency ratio alone does not justify buying more accelerators. The next experiment must measure exposed synchronization, input stalls, and per-device work under the intended batch and checkpoint schedule before choosing an intervention. If convergence changes between configurations, compare complete time-to-quality runs rather than interpreting the difference as pure strong-scaling loss.

A training report can therefore support a configuration choice without claiming that every measured overhead has already been explained. Its acceptance gates concern learning; an efficiently trained model still needs separate evidence that it can serve requests under deployment constraints.

Checkpoint 12.3: Measuring a training claim

The first half of the chapter built the measurement and applied it to training. Before moving to deployment, you should be able to:

Self-Check: Question

  1. How do the primary benchmarking objectives and resource bottlenecks fundamentally differ between training systems and inference serving systems?

    1. Training is latency-critical with millisecond deadlines, whereas inference is throughput-oriented over weeks
    2. Training memory footprint is dominated solely by static weights, whereas inference requires large optimizer states
    3. Training optimizes for sustained throughput (samples/sec) and time-to-quality across accelerator systems with large memory demands (weights, gradients, optimizer states, activations), whereas inference optimizes for latency percentiles (p50, p99), QPS, and energy per query under serving constraints
    4. Training and inference have identical memory access patterns and evaluate the exact same metrics

  2. Why does MLPerf Training mandate time-to-accuracy (or time-to-quality) as its primary benchmark metric instead of raw throughput measured in samples per second?

    1. Samples per second cannot be measured accurately with digital timers
    2. Time-to-accuracy is easier to simulate without running actual GPUs
    3. Hardware vendors do not know the batch size used during training
    4. Raw sample throughput can be artificially inflated by using aggressive low precision or extreme batch sizes that destabilize training, whereas time-to-accuracy ensures throughput optimizations actually converge to the required model quality

  3. A deep learning model trains on a single GPU in \(24\text{ hours}\). With the same total optimizer work, the same training schedule, and the same quality target on the same fixed total dataset (strong scaling), the run completes in \(4\text{ hours}\) on \(8\text{ GPUs}\). What is the strong scaling efficiency, and does this timing result by itself identify the cause of the missing speedup?

    1. \(75\%\) efficiency; profiling is still required to identify the cause of the missing speedup
    2. \(100\%\) efficiency; the run achieved linear speedup because \(24 / 4 = 6\)
    3. \(50\%\) efficiency; GPU memory bandwidth is cut in half whenever multiple GPUs are connected
    4. \(12.5\%\) efficiency; training time only decreased by a factor of 6

  4. Explain why a reduced-precision training configuration (such as FP8 or BF16) that achieves a \(1.8\times\) step-level throughput gain could result in a longer wall-clock time-to-accuracy than standard FP32 training.

  5. In distributed training benchmarks, the scaling regime where the total workload remains constant as the number of accelerator nodes increases is known as ____.

  6. Order the following operational stages of an illustrative successful ML training time-to-accuracy benchmark run from start to finish: (1) Halt training execution and log total elapsed wall-clock time-to-accuracy, (2) Execute distributed forward-backward iterations with gradient synchronization across nodes, (3) Initialize model parameters and data loaders using the run’s documented seed and standardized preprocessing, (4) Check whether the validation accuracy meets or exceeds the declared target quality threshold, (5) Perform periodic evaluation on the held-out validation dataset at predefined intervals

See Answers →

12.6 Inference Benchmarks

Whereas training benchmarks tolerate variable iteration times as long as gradient descent reaches its target quality, inference benchmarks test whether the trained model can serve individual requests within strict deployment deadlines. A camera trap running MobileNetV2 on an edge board must classify each frame within a 33-millisecond budget to keep up with a 30-frame-per-second video stream. If an unbenchmarked garbage collection pause, dynamic batching timeout, or kernel launch hiccup stretches the p99 latency past that deadline, frames are dropped and an animal crossing the field of view in under a second goes unrecorded regardless of the board’s average throughput. An autonomous vehicle’s perception loop runs against the same deadline arithmetic with far higher stakes, because in serving workloads every delayed prediction directly compromises the application.

Inference is also where the optimization chapters converge, because the accelerated hardware runs the compressed models to deliver real-time predictions. Inference benchmarks reveal whether those theoretical speedups become actual latency reductions under realistic deployment conditions. Their central quantity is end-to-end request latency \(\EndToEndRequestLatency\), the full time from a request’s arrival to its response, including queueing, preprocessing, model execution, and postprocessing. It is distinct from the iron law’s latency term \(\Latency\), which counts only the fixed overhead (orchestration, serialization, dispatch) that one execution pays regardless of how much data it moves or arithmetic it performs. Queueing delay, which grows with load, belongs to \(\EndToEndRequestLatency\) but not to \(\Latency\).

Definition 12.4: ML inference benchmarks

ML inference benchmarks are machine learning system benchmarks that quantify a system’s ability to meet latency constraints \((\EndToEndRequestLatency)\) at specified throughput levels, measuring scenario-defined latency statistics, throughput (queries per second), and power efficiency across representative serving scenarios.

  1. Significance: Inference benchmarks expose the workload- and system-specific gap between unconstrained throughput and throughput while meeting a service-level objective (SLO), such as a p99 latency target. Queuing delays push tail latency above the target at high load, and only a benchmark that enforces the target at each throughput level shows it.
  2. Distinction: Unlike training benchmarks, which measure time-to-accuracy over a fixed dataset, inference benchmarks measure per-query response time under realistic load patterns, capturing queuing effects, batching trade-offs, and cold-start overhead that determine real-world serving economics.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that average latency is a sufficient benchmark. A low mean can coexist with a long p99 tail, and at high request rates the slowest 1 percent becomes a large number of affected users. The benchmark must report the statistic the service objective names.

12.6.1 Inference benchmark motivation

Large-scale benchmark training commonly runs on dedicated data center hardware, whereas inference spans a wide range of deployment scenarios, from real-time applications like autonomous driving and conversational AI to mobile devices, IoT systems, and embedded processors. This diversity extends to hardware: while GPUs and TPUs dominate large-scale training, inference workloads often use specialized accelerators like NPUs, field-programmable gate arrays, and dedicated inference chips such as Google’s Edge TPU.19 Inference benchmarks evaluate how well hardware selection, model optimization, and data pipeline design work together across these deployment environments.

19 Edge TPU (tensor processing unit): Google’s fixed-function edge AI accelerator. It illustrates a benchmarking constraint specific to fixed-function accelerators: its headline throughput applies only to quantized TensorFlow Lite models with supported operator types, so models requiring unsupported operators fall back to the host CPU or need graph rewrites before the accelerator result is meaningful.

When models transition from development to these serving targets, the motivating factors parallel those for training (hardware optimization, scalability, cost, fair comparison) but differ in specifics. Software optimization frameworks apply inference-specific techniques such as kernel fusion, precision calibration, and kernel tuning, whose impact on latency, throughput, and power efficiency must be measured under realistic conditions to confirm they deliver real improvements without degrading accuracy. Auto-tuning compilers add a hidden variable. The compiler itself can require hours of optimization per model-hardware pair, so benchmark results reflect the tuning budget as much as the hardware capability. Comparing results across submissions requires normalizing for compiler optimization time.

Scalability concerns also shift character. Training scales by adding GPUs to reduce time-to-accuracy on a fixed workload, whereas inference must scale dynamically in response to fluctuating user demand, handling traffic spikes without violating latency guarantees. Applications that load models on demand, such as serverless deployments, also expose the model’s loading time, its cold start, directly to users.

The cost and energy profile of inference differs sharply from training. Training concentrates cost in discrete runs that may recur as data, objectives, or models change; inference cost accumulates continuously with production traffic. Running an inefficient model at scale can multiply cloud compute expenses, and on battery-powered devices, excessive computation directly affects usability. Benchmarks that measure cost per inference request and efficiency per watt help organizations optimize for both performance and sustainability across deployment platforms.

Holding these diverse serving stacks to reproducible comparisons requires metrics that capture tail responsiveness, streaming token delivery, throughput under load, initialization delay, and energy alongside model quality (Reddi et al. 2019).

12.6.2 Inference metrics

A camera trap must classify each frame before the next one arrives, a voice assistant must respond quickly enough that users do not perceive lag, and a recommendation engine must score enough candidates to keep pace with user scrolling. These constraints (latency and throughput) define the performance envelope within which all serving optimizations must operate. Inference metrics formalize these real-world demands into measurable quantities, and they differ from training metrics in kind rather than degree, because the optimization target shifts from convergence rate during training to latency predictability during serving. Training optimizes for throughput and time-to-accuracy; inference optimizes for latency consistency, resource efficiency, and service-level predictability, spanning cloud data centers handling millions of requests to edge devices operating under strict power constraints.

Latency and tail latency

An inference service that averages 10 ms per request can still time out for thousands of users every hour if queueing bursts or garbage-collection pauses push the slowest 1 percent of requests past its deadline. Latency measures the time for an inference system to process an input and produce a prediction. Because averages conceal those high-percentile delays, benchmarks also report tail latency20—commonly the 95th percentile (p95) or 99th percentile (p99), the time within which 95 percent or 99 percent of inferences complete. Applications with hard deadlines require explicit deadline-miss or worst-case analysis beyond these percentiles. These measurements form the basis for SLOs and SLAs, which formalize performance expectations.

20 Tail latency: A high-percentile response time, such as p95 or p99, determines production SLO or SLA compliance only when that objective or agreement specifies the percentile. Dean and Barroso (2013) showed that in fan-out architectures (common in recommendation systems), even 1 percent slow responses compound: a request touching 100 backend shards has about a 63 percent chance that at least one shard hits its 1 percent tail. Benchmarks reporting only mean latency hide this failure mode.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80. https://doi.org/10.1145/2408776.2408794.
Definition 12.5: SLOs and SLAs

SLOs and SLAs are performance commitment specifications for production ML serving systems: a service-level objective (SLO) is a target value or range for a service-level indicator, while an SLA is a contract that specifies service objectives and the consequences of failing to meet them.

  1. Significance: A latency SLO bounds end-to-end request latency \(\EndToEndRequestLatency\) by setting a target for a specified latency indicator, and the iron law’s \(\Latency\) is only the fixed-overhead part of that budget. Request queueing and transient spikes consume part of that budget.
  2. Distinction: An SLO has no contractual consequence by itself, while missing an SLA objective triggers the consequences specified in the agreement, which may include credits or penalties. Teams commonly set the internal SLO tighter than the external agreement to leave operational headroom, though the definition does not require it.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that meeting average latency satisfies a tail-latency SLO. SLOs can use averages, percentiles, rates, or other indicators; when the objective is defined at p99 or p99.9, an excellent mean does not establish compliance.

Engineering teams optimize toward SLOs while the business commits to SLAs. A usable latency objective also fixes the request population, the offered load, and the measurement window. Otherwise, two teams can report the same percentile threshold over different traffic mixes and reach incompatible conclusions about compliance.

Checkpoint 12.4: Metric selection

The metric shapes the optimization.

Apply three rules before finalizing metric selection:

At production scale, small p99 degradations affect many users. If 1 percent of requests take 10\(\times\) longer (for example, 1000 ms instead of 100 ms), 10,000 requests per million are slow enough to risk timeouts and customer churn. Search engines and recommendation systems demonstrate this sensitivity: Google’s search-latency experiments found measurable reductions in daily searches per user after 100–400 ms server-side delays (Brutlag 2009), which is why interactive services often treat sub-100 ms response times as a practical design target. Because occasional slow responses weigh heavily on user satisfaction, such services define percentile-based objectives, and large-scale systems may track deeper tails such as p99.9.

Brutlag, Jake. 2009. Speed Matters for Google Web Search. Google Research Blog.

↪ Provisioning serving capacity so that p99 latency meets its SLO under peak load is developed in Tail latency.

The challenge of meeting these tail latency targets is that the source of the tail is often architectural, not algorithmic. A garbage-collected runtime, a shared kernel driver, or a priority-inversion bug in the serving stack can inject latency spikes that no model optimization will remove.

Discord’s Read States service shows the effect outside machine learning. Periodic garbage-collection pauses in its Go implementation spiked p99 latency every two minutes until a rewrite in Rust removed the pauses (Howarth 2020). A managed runtime in a feature store or retrieval path can impose the same floor on an ML service’s tail, and no model optimization removes it, which is why the complete serving stack belongs inside a production latency benchmark. Tail latency develops the mechanisms that shape the tail under load.

Howarth, Jesse. 2020. “Why Discord Is Switching from Go to Rust.”

Token latency: TTFT and TPOT

Generative models are served one token at a time, so their latency splits into two measured quantities. Time to first token (TTFT) is the interval from a request’s arrival to its first output token, dominated by prefill, the pass that processes the whole prompt in parallel. Time per output token (TPOT) is the mean interval between subsequent tokens, set by autoregressive decode. The gap between any two successive tokens is the inter-token latency (ITL), so TPOT is the average of a request’s ITLs, and the ITL distribution exposes the individual stalls that the average hides. A benchmark that reports a single aggregate throughput hides both. A model can achieve high throughput in tokens per second while still feeling sluggish if the first token arrives late. It can also minimize TTFT while generating subsequent text too slowly for interactive reading.

Token throughput translates these generation rates into user-perceived wall-clock time: for a response of 750 tokens, a generation rate of 25 tokens/s requires 30 seconds, whereas 100 tokens/s finishes in 7.5 seconds. Time-to-first-token and inter-token latency must therefore be reported together: one captures initial responsiveness, and the other captures sustained delivery speed. This latency split reflects contrasting computational regimes. Time-to-first-token is governed by prompt prefill, which processes tokens in parallel and is typically compute-bound. Inter-token latency is governed by autoregressive decoding, where low-batch execution often streams weights and accesses a growing KV cache with limited reuse. The resulting bandwidth pressure depends on model residency, context length, batch size, and the execution path. Performance metrics: TTFT and TPOT builds serving policies on these two measurements; the benchmarking requirement is that both are reported, under the same offered load, for every generative workload.

Throughput and resource metrics

Latency is only half of the serving envelope. Throughput counts how many inference requests a system completes per second, typically expressed as queries per second (QPS) or frames per second (FPS), and it is meaningful only together with the latency at which it was measured. Batching raises throughput by processing several inputs at once while adding wait time to each, so a throughput figure reported without its latency percentile and offered load says nothing about whether a serving deadline is met. A benchmark therefore reports batch throughput and per-request latency as a pair at the same offered load. Choosing the batch size that balances them is a serving policy (Formation delay and the latency-throughput frontier); the benchmark’s job is to measure both sides of that trade.

Speed alone is insufficient because inference optimizations can change model behavior. Reducing numerical precision can accelerate computation while cutting memory and energy, as the simplified MobileNetV2 energy estimate worked out in the power section shows (section 12.7.1), but lower-precision calculations can introduce accuracy degradation. Inference benchmarks therefore evaluate how well models perform under different numerical settings, such as FP32, FP16, and INT8.21 Model compression techniques22 further improve efficiency, but their impact on model accuracy varies depending on the task and dataset. Benchmarks help determine whether these optimizations are viable for deployment, ensuring that improvements in efficiency do not come at the cost of unacceptable accuracy loss.

21 INT8 (8-bit integer): INT8 sits at the aggressive end of the precision hierarchy (FP32 baseline, FP16 halves raw weight storage, INT8 quarters it), and each step demands increasing care to preserve accuracy. Post-training INT8 quantization normally fits its value ranges on a representative calibration dataset (quantization-range calibration, distinct from the confidence calibration of section 12.9.1.2); accuracy depends on the model, operator coverage, quantization method, and how well the calibration data represent deployment inputs. INT8 benchmarks must specify whether they use post-training quantization or quantization-aware training and document any range-calibration procedure.

22 Model compression benchmarking: Compression impact must be measured across four dimensions simultaneously: accuracy degradation, inference speedup, memory reduction, and energy savings. A technique achieving 10\(\times\) size reduction with 1 percent accuracy loss may still be unsuitable if latency misses the deployment target; unstructured pruning, for example, leaves dense execution unchanged unless a compatible sparse representation and kernel exploit the zeros.

23 Cold-start latency: The initialization time from idle state includes storage reads, host-to-accelerator transfer, and framework setup. For a 7B-parameter model in FP16 (~14 GB) already resident in host memory, PCIe 4.0 transfer at 25 GB/s effective bandwidth alone takes ~560 ms; storage and initialization add to it. This lower bound makes cold-start mitigation (model caching, speculative loading) a systems design requirement.

24 Serverless AI: Deployment paradigm where models scale from zero instances on demand. Cold-start time varies with the model, runtime, storage path, hardware allocation, and provider. Benchmarks for intermittent workloads must report whether initialization and model loading are included, because warm-instance latency alone can understate user-perceived latency.

Beyond speed and accuracy, table 15 lists the resource terms an inference benchmark must also report, and each exists because a system can pass the latency test and still fail to deploy. Memory footprint decides whether the model fits the device’s budget at all, since inference rarely spans accelerators the way training can. Cold-start performance, the load time plus first-request latency,23 exposes initialization cost to the requesting user. It dominates in serverless AI environments24 and on devices that evict models to free memory. When provisioning, model transfer, runtime initialization, and the first response lie on the user request path, the benchmark must include them; a warm-instance result answers a different question. Scalability records whether added accelerators or concurrent models raise throughput or collide on memory bandwidth, and energy per inference (joules per inference, or QPS/W in the cloud) sets battery life on mobile devices and operating cost in data centers.

12.6.3 MLPerf inference benchmarks

A serving score is meaningful only against the constraints of its target environment because a data-center rack, a client laptop, a smartphone, and a microcontroller cannot be judged by one test. Developed by MLCommons,25 the MLPerf inference family turns the balance among latency, throughput, memory, energy, and accuracy into scored scenarios matched to each deployment tier, so a result can be interpreted against the resource limits the deployed system will face.

25 MLCommons: Launched in 2020 as a nonprofit consortium evolving from the 2018 MLPerf effort, MLCommons includes members across industry and academia (MLCommons 2026a). Requiring published system specifications improves result comparability, but it does not prevent submitters from selecting which systems or workloads to enter. Published results reveal large performance differences between vendors on identical workloads, making MLCommons the closest the field has to SPEC-style like-for-like hardware comparison.

MLCommons. 2026a. MLCommons: About Us.

MLPerf Inference

At the data-center and edge appliance tier, the baseline MLPerf Inference suite (Reddi et al. 2019) scores submissions under the closed and open divisions defined in section 12.4.4. Its workloads span image classification, object detection, natural language processing, and recommendation, providing a common reference point for comparing accelerators and CPUs when the submission rules and scenario match the intended deployment. Because different workloads stress different hardware subsystems, scores on DLRM26 can separate accelerator generations on memory capacity and bandwidth where compute-bound vision models cannot, although the size of the gain depends on workload, software stack, and system configuration. At the same time, the cost of comprehensive benchmarking limits who can participate and how thoroughly systems are evaluated.

26 DLRM (deep learning recommendation model): Facebook’s 2019 recommendation architecture combines embedding tables for categorical features with multilayer perceptrons for continuous features (Naumov et al. 2019). DLRM stresses benchmarks differently than vision or language models: its embedding tables can be large enough that memory capacity and bandwidth dominate compute throughput. That makes DLRM the book’s capacity-bound workload in MLPerf-style inference evaluation, revealing hardware limitations invisible to compute-bound benchmarks (Reddi et al. 2019).

Naumov, Maxim, Dheevatsa Mudigere, Hao-Jun Michael Shi, Jianyu Huang, Narayanan Sundaraman, Jongsoo Park, Xiaodong Wang, et al. 2019. “Deep Learning Recommendation Model for Personalization and Recommendation Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1906.00091.
Reddi, Vijay Janapa, Christine Cheng, David Kanter, Peter Mattson, Guenther Schmuelling, Carole-Jean Wu, Brian Anderson, et al. 2019. “MLPerf Inference Benchmark.” 2020 ACM/IEEE 47th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA), 446–59. https://doi.org/10.1109/isca45697.2020.00045.

Systems Perspective 12.6: The cost of comprehensive benchmarking
Submitting to MLPerf can require dedicated engineering effort, hardware access, tuning, validation, and careful documentation across the relevant configurations. The cost depends heavily on workload and scope, but it is high enough that submissions are dominated by major technology companies and hardware vendors, while smaller organizations rely on published results rather than conducting their own comprehensive evaluations. That barrier motivates the need for more lightweight, internal benchmarking practices that organizations can use to make informed decisions without the expense of full-scale standardized benchmarking.

The rest of the MLPerf inference family narrows that baseline as power and memory budgets tighten. MLPerf Mobile (MLCommons 2024a) evaluates whether a model stays responsive within smartphone power and memory limits (Janapa Reddi et al. 2022). MLPerf Client (MLCommons 2026b) asks whether consumer devices can run local generative-AI workloads across CPUs, discrete GPUs, and integrated NPUs. MLPerf Tiny (Banbury et al. 2021) covers embedded and ultra-low-power systems, such as IoT devices, wearables, and microcontrollers. Moving from data-center servers to these mobile and edge targets changes the physics of the measurement itself, because passive cooling and battery limits turn thermal throttling and cross-engine transfers into primary bottlenecks.

MLCommons. 2024a. MLPerf Mobile Benchmark.
Janapa Reddi, Vijay et al. 2022. “MLPerf Mobile V2. 0: An Industry-Standard Benchmark Suite for Mobile Machine Learning.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 4: 806–23.
MLCommons. 2026b. MLPerf Client Benchmark.

12.6.4 Mobile and edge benchmarks

Unlike data centers with kilowatt-scale power delivery and active cooling infrastructure, mobile and edge deployments operate within strict milliwatt- to watt-scale power envelopes and passive thermal dissipation limits. Benchmarking these systems means reporting power consumption, inference latency, and model accuracy together, because gains in one usually come at the expense of another. Table 13 contrasts these operational constraints against cloud environments.

Consider a smartphone camera executing real-time object detection at 30 frames per second within a 3 W to 5 W thermal envelope. In this setting, an efficient architecture such as MobileNet serves as a realistic benchmark target where a larger ResNet model would violate thermal limits, despite the larger model’s superior accuracy in cloud evaluations. An edge benchmark must therefore evaluate accuracy jointly with sustained power and thermal dissipation. The peak-versus-sustained gap (section 12.1) is especially acute at the edge, because a passively cooled device sheds heat poorly. As junction temperatures rise, the dynamic thermal management (DTM) controller scales down operating voltage and clock frequency, causing sustained throughput to drop sharply below burst-mode peaks. That thermal mechanism makes the cooling configuration part of the benchmark specification.

Example 12.2: Benchmarking the edge
Scenario: An edge engineering team benchmarks a candidate chip for the camera trap’s board with a vendor-advertised “1 W AI” power specification.

Diagnosis: Early burst-mode testing matches vendor claims, but continuous inference loop execution builds up junction heat, triggering thermal throttling and dropping steady-state throughput.

Systems lesson: Short burst benchmarks obscure long-term thermal throttling. Benchmarking edge ML workloads requires continuous, sustained execution testing to reflect true operational hardware limits.

Burst benchmark FPS collapses once thermal throttling sets in.

Throttling is the most visible way a short measurement flatters edge hardware. Idle power, duty cycle, and the data movement around the accelerator can distort a headline number too, so each needs checking before a vendor claim informs a design decision.

Systems Perspective 12.7: Edge benchmark reality check

When evaluating edge hardware claims, four factors determine whether vendor numbers translate to real-world performance:

  1. Peak vs. sustained: A vendor may advertise 45 TOPS peak throughput while a sustained thermal run delivers closer to 20 TOPS. In this scenario, use a sustained run longer than 30 s and verify thermal stabilization; also test the deployment duty cycle.
  2. Power at idle vs. active: In this scenario, a device consuming 50 mW idle and 2 W active could report active draw for marketing, but if the application runs inference 1 percent of the time, effective power draw is ~69.5 mW, not 2 W.
  3. Thermal envelope: Edge devices often operate inside a narrow TDP envelope. Exceeding it triggers throttling, so benchmark reports omitting thermal conditions are incomplete.
  4. End-to-end vs. accelerator-only: NPU benchmarks often exclude data transfer overhead. Moving image data from camera to NPU and back can exceed inference time for small models.

A modern mobile SoC packages CPUs, GPUs, digital signal processors (DSPs), and neural processing units (NPUs) on one die that shares system memory and a common thermal budget (Heterogeneous SoC Design develops how these SoCs partition work). An edge benchmark must therefore measure the pipeline across those engines rather than one engine’s kernel throughput. Single-engine benchmarks miss three costs that fall between the engines, namely waking an accelerator from a deep sleep state, contention when several engines access shared DRAM, and converting tensor layouts between the formats different engines require. Tracing how an isolated accelerator speedup shrinks once host preprocessing and cross-engine transfers are included is the job of the camera-trap board benchmark that follows.

12.6.5 Building the edge-board benchmark

The camera trap’s board is the case for the inference measurements above. It pairs a low-power host CPU with an attached INT8 accelerator, and the claim inherited from the compression and acceleration chapters is that INT8 MobileNetV2 on the accelerator meets the camera’s frame deadline at a fraction of the host’s energy. The benchmark is built in the order a careful engineer would follow: precision on the host first, then the accelerator, then the gates that decide whether the result can ship. The labeled composite values below are illustrative, not measurements of a named product.

Example 12.3: Benchmarking a vision model for edge deployment
Scenario: An edge AI team evaluates MobileNetV2 on the camera trap’s host CPU, first at FP32 and then at INT8, under strict latency and model size limits (Sandler et al. 2018).

Diagnosis: FP32 execution yields 50 ms latency (20 FPS) and 14 MB size. INT8 quantization speeds up inference by 3.3× to 15 ms (66.7 FPS) and reduces model size by 4×, at a cost of 0.9 percentage points Top-1 accuracy.

Systems lesson: Edge model benchmarking requires evaluating multi-dimensional trade-offs across latency, accuracy, and memory payload. Quantization enables high-throughput edge execution when modest accuracy drops meet product requirements.

Lighthouse 12.2: MobileNetV2 on the edge accelerator
Extending lighthouse 12.1, the hardware-acceleration claim is evaluated using an illustrative SingleStream-style edge-inference scenario (Reddi et al. 2019; Banbury et al. 2021).

Hardware acceleration claim: In this illustrative edge-accelerator scenario, assume INT8 MobileNetV2 inference takes ~2 ms on the accelerator, approximately 7.5× faster than the same board’s low-power host CPU (~15 ms). The host CPU column is the INT8 host result of example 12.3, so both measurements describe the same board. Actual results depend on operator coverage, clock frequency, thermal state, and implementation.

Table 14 reports the illustrative SingleStream-style scenario assumptions across distinct measurement scopes.

What this reveals: Under these assumptions, the 7.5× inference speedup narrows to ~3× end to end because preprocessing runs on the host CPU in both cases (figure 8). The accelerator path incurs additional latency copying each input across the host-accelerator link and retrieving the result, a transfer overhead the CPU-only path never pays.

Furthermore, the 1.8× energy advantage applies specifically to the active inference interval (\(\ActiveInferencePower \times \InferenceLatency\)), measuring component-level efficiency rather than total system consumption. This metric excludes host CPU preprocessing energy, interconnect transmission power, and baseline idle draw during non-inference phases. Because end-to-end latency improves by only 3×, host baseline power consumed during these non-accelerated phases narrows the whole-system battery efficiency gain.

Workload arrival patterns determine whether component-level energy efficiency translates to extended battery life. For intermittent duty cycles, the lower active inference energy favors the accelerator, but total battery drain depends on sleep power, wake-up latency and energy overhead, host transfer costs, and any standby leakage the accelerator draws while awaiting work. For continuous streaming workloads such as video analytics, the accelerator’s lower active energy per inference dominates cumulative energy consumption.

The SingleStream result illustrates why benchmarking requires matching the MLPerf scenario to the deployment context: SingleStream emphasizes latency, while Offline benchmarks would optimize for bulk throughput rather than latency bounds.

Banbury, Colby, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Peter Torelli, Jeremy Holleman, Nat Jeffries, Csaba Kiraly, Pietro Montino, et al. 2021. “MLPerf Tiny Benchmark.” arXiv Preprint.

The board’s INT8 results support further evaluation, but they do not establish that the camera trap’s service objective has been met. The decision requires the request population, latency statistic, quality floor, and memory and energy budgets to be specified together.

Table 15 identifies the remaining evidence. A camera pipeline needs sustained completion times under its input cadence; an intermittent application also needs initialization and sleep-state costs. For either application, the candidate must retain the required model quality on representative inputs. The same model-level speedup can therefore support one deployment and fail another.

The acceptance decision can reject a candidate on any binding constraint. A lower model-only latency does not establish an end-to-end gain if preprocessing dominates, and a smaller weight file does not establish that runtime buffers fit the device. The latency decomposition and Amdahl calculation in section 12.3.4 specify which additional measurements the MobileNetV2 comparison needs.

Because physical constraints differ across environments, no single metric ordering governs all systems. Benchmarking requires establishing a priority hierarchy derived directly from the deployment specification. Table 16 illustrates how metric priorities shift across five operational contexts, mapping hardware and operational constraints to optimization targets.

A metric that dictates architectural success in one deployment context may be irrelevant in another. Latency ranks first for autonomous robotics, where sensor-processing pipelines must complete within strict execution deadlines to maintain vehicle stability, whereas cloud batch services accept higher queuing latency in exchange for lower operational cost per query. A 2\(\times\) throughput gain represents substantial economic value in a hyperscale cloud cluster, but it provides little benefit on a battery-powered device such as the camera trap, where a 20 percent power reduction extends operational lifetime.

For the camera example, use the required deadline or percentile as the timing gate, the runtime peak allocation as the capacity gate, and the permitted quality loss as the model gate. If any gate lacks a measurement, the decision remains unresolved even when the reported inference speedup is large. A protocol should identify the missing evidence rather than treating the favorable metric as approval. An on-demand deployment adds a fourth gate, cold-start latency, measured on the user request path as section 12.6.2.3 specifies.

Those quality and timing gates also explain why a vendor’s headline INT8 operation rate cannot substitute for running the model on the board. Numerical precision optimization introduces a comparability trap across accelerator platforms because accelerators advertise substantially higher INT8 throughput27 than baseline FP32 rates, yet those peak figures translate into usable board performance only when the benchmark verifies accuracy retention under quantization noise, confirms hardware support across all model operators without host fallbacks, and enforces equivalent operation definitions across architectures.

27 TOPS (tera operations per second): A measure of raw computational throughput (trillions of operations/second). The H100 delivers 1979 TOPS INT8 vs. the Apple M2 Neural Engine at 15.8 TOPS and Edge TPU at 4 TOPS, but these numbers conflate different operation types (multiply-accumulate, accumulate, or activation). TOPS comparisons across vendors are meaningful only when the operation definition, precision, and sparsity assumptions are identical, conditions rarely met in vendor specifications.

Once a candidate clears quality and capacity gates, varying offered load tests how far its timing result transfers. A runtime can sustain a favorable latency with one request in flight yet miss the target when queues and shared memory fill. The relevant outcome is the supported load under the declared latency bound, measured with the same input mix and power policy, rather than an isolated peak throughput.

The board now has a latency, a throughput, and a memory footprint. What it lacks is the number that decides whether a battery-powered camera trap lasts a season in the field: the energy each frame costs.

Self-Check: Question

  1.
