12.7 Power and Energy Measurement

Estimating that per-frame energy cost from a vendor datasheet is unreliable because energy is the one benchmark quantity that cannot be read off a clock or inferred from isolated peak ratings. A chip vendor may advertise “10 TOPS at 0.5 W,” yet under sustained inference load, thermal throttling drops actual throughput to 3 TOPS at 2 W. That 13.3× efficiency gap between the datasheet and reality goes undetected until deployment unless power is integrated over a stated measurement boundary and window under load. For a duty-cycled device, total energy also depends on how the hardware spends the idle intervals between inferences. KWS, the book’s power-bound lighthouse, carries the measurement of energy in this section because a wake-word detector is judged by how long it listens on a battery rather than by how fast it answers, while the edge board’s frames and the data center’s racks serve as high-duty-cycle contrasts.

This efficiency dimension matters directly because Anatomy of an Accelerator defines acceleration by its compute-density and performance-per-watt gains, making TOPS/W a primary design objective alongside raw TOPS. Power benchmarks validate whether efficiency-optimized accelerators deliver their promised energy savings. TOPS/W is particularly susceptible to gaming because it is a ratio of two separately quotable peaks. A vendor can read the numerator (operations) at the batch size and precision that maximize throughput and the denominator (watts) at a near-idle operating point. The advertised efficiency then describes a state the chip never occupies under real load. Power benchmarks close that loophole by fixing the workload and the measurement window, forcing the numerator and denominator to be read at the same operating point.

Datasheet TOPS/W overstates sustained efficiency by 13.3×.

Measuring power differs from measuring execution time because power is not a single timestamped interval: instantaneous power (supply voltage times current) fluctuates continuously across microsecond execution phases and drifts over minutes as silicon heats up, increasing temperature-dependent leakage current. ML deployments also span a wide physical range. The dumbbell plot in figure 9 isolates this disparity on a logarithmic power scale. It plots minimum (blue) and maximum (red) envelopes across four deployment tiers: TinyML systems (5.6 mW to 167 mW), edge hardware (3.9 W to 1.1 kW), data center inference nodes (267 W to 6.3 kW), and large-scale training clusters (5.5 kW to 498 kW). Spanning about eight orders of magnitude from the TinyML floor to the training-cluster ceiling, this spectrum explains why no single efficiency metric serves all deployment contexts. A figure of merit optimized for multi-rack data center throughput becomes meaningless for a battery-powered keyword spotter governed by milliwatt budgets.

Because measured power spans from milliwatts to hundreds of kilowatts, no single instrument or measurement protocol applies everywhere. Benchmarking an always-on keyword spotter requires measuring microampere currents at millivolt rails, whereas benchmarking a server rack requires measuring high-voltage three-phase alternating current at the power distribution unit. This divergence forces benchmarking standards to establish precise physical boundaries for what hardware is included in the measured energy.

Energy accounting follows contrasting regimes across training and inference. Training energy is a capital investment amortized across the operational lifetime of the model; a large training run can consume on the order of a thousand megawatt-hours (section 12.5.2.4) (Patterson et al. 2021). Inference energy accumulates continuously with request volume, dominating total lifecycle cost as query counts scale into millions. For an isolated inference window containing one completed request, energy obeys the identity \(\InferenceEnergy= \ActiveInferencePower\times \InferenceLatency\), with \(\ActiveInferencePower\) the average accelerator power during that window and \(\InferenceLatency\) its duration. The duty-cycle form used for battery accounting appears in section 12.7.2. With batching or concurrent requests, integrate energy over a common measurement window and divide by completed requests; multiplying total device power by each request’s overlapping serving latency would double-count energy. For example, if measured average accelerator power during the inference window is 300 W, a 10 ms inference uses \(\text{300 W} \times \text{0.01 s} = \text{3 J}\), or about 0.0008 Wh; at 100 ms, that becomes about 0.0083 Wh. An accelerator’s TDP rating represents a cooling ceiling rather than dynamic power consumption, making empirical runtime power measurement essential for accurate benchmarking.

Patterson, David, Joseph Gonzalez, Quoc Le, Chen Liang, Lluis-Miquel Munguia, Daniel Rothchild, David So, Maud Texier, and Jeff Dean. 2021. “Carbon Emissions and Large Neural Network Training.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2104.10350.

12.7.1 Power measurement boundaries

Where an engineer draws the system boundary dictates what counts as efficient. If a benchmark measures only the accelerator core, it ignores the host CPU driving the PCIe bus, the off-chip DRAM holding weights, and the cooling fans dissipating heat. Figure 10 partitions these measurement perimeters across three deployment tiers (Tiny, Inference, and Training), where solid olive enclosures demarcate measured components and red dashed boxes isolate excluded infrastructure such as remote storage and data-center chillers (Tschand et al. 2024).

The three architectural tiers in figure 10 (Tiny Example, Inference Example, and Training Example) reflect distinct physical instrumentation points. In TinyML deployments, the entire low-power SoC, including compute cores, internal SRAM, and power management units, falls inside the boundary, measured directly at the DC power rail. Dedicated inference nodes expand this perimeter to include host CPUs, accelerator cards, PCIe buses, local storage, and on-chassis active cooling fans, while excluding remote storage networks. Multi-rack training deployments measure compute nodes and top-of-rack interconnect switches, but exclude centralized data center chillers, facility-level power distribution units (PDUs), and disaggregated storage clusters.

The excluded components can more than double the reported energy. Google measured the energy of the median text prompt served by its Gemini assistant under two boundaries (Elsworth et al. 2025). Counting only the active accelerators, on prompts served by its most efficient data centers, gave 0.10 Wh. Counting everything the service draws to answer that prompt gave 0.24 Wh, about 2.4× more. Of that total, the active accelerators drew 58 percent and the host CPUs and DRAM that feed them 25 percent. The remaining 17 percent went to machines held idle in reserve for traffic spikes and to facility overhead such as cooling and power conversion. An accelerator-only figure therefore understates what a deployed service consumes, and two figures measured under different boundaries are not comparable. Utilization also moves the figure, because the narrow measurement came from the most efficiently used machines. Open benchmark suites such as ML.ENERGY measure energy under realistic serving conditions so that their results reflect deployed utilization rather than an idealized run (Chung et al. 2025).

Elsworth, Cooper, Keguo Huang, David Patterson, Ian Schneider, Robert Sedivy, Savannah Goodman, Ben Townsend, et al. 2025. “Measuring the Environmental Impact of Delivering AI at Google Scale.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2508.15734.
Chung, Jae-Won, Jeff J. Ma, Ruofan Wu, Jiachen Liu, Oh Jun Kweon, Yuxuan Xia, Zhiyu Wu, and Mosharaf Chowdhury. 2025. “The ML.ENERGY Benchmark: Toward Automated Inference Energy Measurement and Optimization.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS), Datasets and Benchmarks Track.
Boroumand, Amirali, Saugata Ghose, Youngsok Kim, Rachata Ausavarungnirun, Eric Shiu, Rahul Thakur, Daehyun Kim, et al. 2018. “Google Workloads for Consumer Devices: Mitigating Data Movement Bottlenecks.” Proceedings of the Twenty-Third International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems, ASPLOS ’18, 316–31. https://doi.org/10.1145/3173162.3173177.

The measurement boundary determines which components are counted; workload composition determines where their energy is spent. On mobile devices running TensorFlow Mobile, data movement accounts for 57.3 percent of total inference energy (Boroumand et al. 2018), exposing why operation counts alone fail to predict power consumption. Under the energy-movement principle (7), total energy depends on how many events of each kind occur and what each one costs, not on arithmetic alone. The worked example in notebook 12.4 decomposes MobileNetV2 inference energy to demonstrate how moving from FP32 to INT8 attacks both the memory-movement bottleneck and arithmetic switching costs.

Napkin Math 12.4: Why INT8 saves energy

Problem: Under the stated 45 nm component-energy model, how much does replacing FP32 with INT8 reduce MobileNetV2 weight-read and arithmetic energy?

Moving data costs far more energy than computing on it (principle 7, grounded in Horowitz’s energy estimates (Horowitz 2014)). Understanding why quantization reduces energy consumption requires decomposing energy into its physical sources. Two dominant factors determine inference energy: compute operations and memory access.

Narrower datatypes require less switching and storage energy per operation, and off-chip memory costs far more than either. In the 45 nm component model that Precision and energy tabulates (INT8 versus FP32 Energy per Operation), an 8-bit multiplier uses about 18.5× less energy than a 32-bit floating-point multiplier. Reading one byte from DRAM, about 160 pJ/byte, costs over 1,600× more than a register access (Horowitz 2014). Absolute values and ratios vary with circuit design and process technology.

Math: Component energy follows the energy corollary, with a load term \(\DataVolume \times \EnergyPerByteMoved\) (model bytes times DRAM energy per byte) and a compute term \(\Operations\times \EnergyPerOperation\) (operations times energy per operation). The FP32 terms sum as 2243 µJ + 2,220 µJ = 4,463 µJ; the INT8 terms sum as 561 µJ + 120 µJ = 681 µJ. Dividing the totals gives a 6.6× reduction under this simplified model.

Table 17 combines the two effects in a deliberately simplified MobileNetV2 model. It counts one DRAM read per weight and charges every cataloged FLOP at the listed multiplier energy, while excluding activation traffic, cache behavior, additions as a distinct cost, control overhead, and static power:

Systems insight: Within this simplified model, the weight read and the arithmetic contribute comparable FP32 energy (2243 µJ and 2,220 µJ), and INT8 reduces both, taking the total from 4,463 µJ to 681 µJ. The decomposition explains the physical mechanism behind potential savings, but battery-life or per-inference claims require controlled whole-device power measurements.

Horowitz, Mark. 2014. “1.1 Computing’s Energy Problem (and What We Can Do about It).” 2014 IEEE International Solid-State Circuits Conference Digest of Technical Papers (ISSCC), 10–14. https://doi.org/10.1109/isscc.2014.6757323.
Barroso, Luiz André, Urs Hölzle, and Parthasarathy Ranganathan. 2019. The Datacenter as a Computer: Designing Warehouse-Scale Machines. Synthesis Lectures on Computer Architecture. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-01761-2.
Kim, Wonyoung, Meeta S. Gupta, Gu-Yeon Wei, and David Brooks. 2008. “System Level Analysis of Fast, Per-Core DVFS Using on-Chip Switching Regulators.” IEEE International Symposium on High-Performance Computer Architecture (HPCA), HPCA ’08, 123–34. https://doi.org/10.1109/hpca.2008.4658633.

Beyond individual server nodes, large-scale deployments introduce shared infrastructure that complicates boundary definition. In data centers, cooling systems and power delivery account for 20 to 30 percent of total facility electricity consumption (Barroso et al. 2019). This facility-level overhead is captured by power usage effectiveness (PUE): \[\text{PUE} = \frac{\text{Total Facility Energy}}{\text{IT Equipment Energy}}\] PUE values range from approximately 1.1 in hyperscale facilities using direct-to-chip liquid cooling to over 2.0 in legacy facilities relying on computer room air conditioning (Barroso et al. 2019). When an evaluation reports only server-plug energy, it excludes this facility multiplier. Furthermore, dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) continuously modulates processor power and heat dissipation during execution (Kim et al. 2008), creating thermal feedback loops that alter the cooling overhead itself. On untethered edge and tiny devices, the boundary moves from the facility plug to the battery rail, where device lifetime depends on how active bursts, idle intervals, and rising junction temperatures accumulate over time.

12.7.2 Battery and thermal benchmarking

Battery drain depends on total energy consumed over time rather than peak instantaneous power. Computational photography bursts consume multiple watts for hundreds of milliseconds during image capture, whereas background sensor processing must remain within a milliwatt-scale budget to deliver multi-day battery life. Characterizing battery impact requires measuring the total energy consumption \(\TotalEnergy = \int \DevicePower(\TimeInstant)\,d\TimeInstant\) across representative operational profiles, where \(\DevicePower(\TimeInstant)\) is the instantaneous power the device draws at time \(\TimeInstant\).

The dominant operational variable is the workload duty cycle \(\InferenceDutyCycle\), defined as the fraction of time the accelerator actively runs inference. For an active power draw \(\ActiveInferencePower\) and standby power draw \(\StandbyPower\), the average power is: \[\AveragePower= \InferenceDutyCycle \ActiveInferencePower+ (1 - \InferenceDutyCycle) \StandbyPower\] For intermittent workloads such as the camera trap waiting for motion (\(\InferenceDutyCycle\ll 0.01\)), standby power \(\StandbyPower\) and memory retention power dictate battery lifetime, making active inference efficiency secondary. For continuous pipelines like video analytics (\(\InferenceDutyCycle\approx 1.0\)), active inference energy \(\InferenceEnergy= \ActiveInferencePower\cdot \InferenceLatency\) dominates. Furthermore, elevated operating temperatures increase semiconductor leakage current, causing static power dissipation to climb as the device heats up. Benchmarking battery impact therefore requires measuring both active and standby power across the operational duty cycle while monitoring junction temperature over extended multi-minute runs.

Keyword spotting makes the point concrete. Run DS-CNN (Zhang et al. 2017), the MLPerf Tiny keyword-spotting reference at 20 MFLOP per inference, on an ESP32-S3-class microcontroller board with one inference per one-second audio window. At the board’s peak INT8 rate an inference takes about 8.3 ms, a duty cycle of about 0.8 percent, and costs about 3.3 mJ at the board’s 0.4 W active power. Between inferences the board idles at 50 mW. Average power is therefore about 52.9 mW, and 94 percent of it is spent waiting. A benchmark that reports only energy per inference describes the remaining 6 percent of the battery drain. Active energy would overtake the idle floor only if inference occupied more than about 11 percent of the time, and real kernels running below peak raise the active share without changing the conclusion. This is why always-on audio moves to dedicated low-power silicon whose idle draw is microwatts, and why a power benchmark for a duty-cycled device must specify the duty cycle along with the inference.

Zhang, Yundong, Naveen Suda, Liangzhen Lai, and Vikas Chandra. 2017. Hello Edge: Keyword Spotting on Microcontrollers.

12.7.3 Computational efficiency vs. power consumption

Even when the workload and duty cycle are fixed, a short boost-clock burst and a warm sustained run report different efficiencies for the same chip. That shift follows from CMOS physics, since total power is dynamic switching power plus static leakage power, and over the range where supply voltage tracks clock frequency, dynamic power grows roughly with the cube of frequency (Power and thermal management derives both). Because a few percent of clock frequency moves power far more than throughput, a measured efficiency belongs strictly to the operating point at which it was taken. Static leakage also rises with temperature, making a warm device less efficient than a cold one at the same clock, which is why the power protocol below requires warming the device to its intended thermal state before logging begins. Across hardware generations, computations per kilowatt-hour historically doubled approximately every 1.5 years, a long-run trend known as Koomey’s law (Koomey et al. 2011), so an efficiency comparison across devices must also state which generation each one represents.

Koomey, Jonathan, Stephen Berard, Marla Sanchez, and Henry Wong. 2011. “Implications of Historical Trends in the Electrical Efficiency of Computing.” IEEE Annals of the History of Computing 33 (3): 46–54. https://doi.org/10.1109/mahc.2010.28.
Wu, Hao, Patrick Judd, Xiaojie Zhang, Mikhail Isaev, and Paulius Micikevicius. 2020. “Integer Quantization for Deep Learning Inference: Principles and Empirical Evaluation.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2004.09602 abs/2004.09602.
Gholami, Amir, Sehoon Kim, Zhen Dong, Zhewei Yao, Michael W. Mahoney, and Kurt Keutzer. 2021. “A Survey of Quantization Methods for Efficient Neural Network Inference.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2103.13630 abs/2103.13630.

Because frequency scaling quickly hits TDP ceilings, system designers optimize efficiency across the Data, Algorithm, and Machine axes. On the algorithm and data side, reduced-precision arithmetic (INT8 or FP8) directly reduces the capacitance switched in the ALUs and compresses memory traffic across interconnect buses, potentially cutting dynamic energy when the chosen format preserves the required model quality (Jacob et al. 2018; Wu et al. 2020; Gholami et al. 2021). On the machine side, the energy-delay product (EDP) measures one trade-off between energy and completion time. Its minimum need not coincide with minimum energy or maximum throughput per watt. Battery-constrained devices and power-limited data centers must choose operating points against their own latency, throughput, and energy requirements, using calibrated meters and synchronized measurement windows that capture leakage, active switching, and idle power together.

12.7.4 Standardized power measurement

General-purpose power benchmarks such as SPECpower_ssj2008 measure server power across stepped load levels (0 to 100 percent in 10 percent increments) using steady-state transactional workloads (Lange 2009). In contrast, ML workloads swing rapidly in power: instantaneous draw alternates between compute-bound matrix multiplications (where systolic arrays or Tensor Cores draw peak current) and memory-bound stalls or token transfers (where execution units stall waiting for DRAM or interconnect fetches).

Lange, Klaus-Dieter. 2009. “Identifying Shades of Green: The SPECpower Benchmarks.” IEEE Computer 42 (3): 95–97. https://doi.org/10.1109/mc.2009.84.

Characterizing these fluctuations requires calibrated physical instrumentation. Software interfaces such as Intel RAPL and NVIDIA NVML expose counters or telemetry with device-specific accuracy, update rates, and polling overhead. The instrument must match the measurement boundary and required uncertainty. MLPerf power protocols use external calibrated analyzers for inference systems, while training and HPC measurements can use validated node or power-distribution telemetry; Tiny measurements use instrumentation suited to low-power devices (Tschand et al. 2024).

Resolving an individual kernel transient requires instrument bandwidth and sampling fast enough to follow it, because a kilohertz log cannot trace microsecond power spikes. Total run energy instead requires integrating power over the entire measurement window: \[\TotalEnergy = \int_{t_{\text{start}}}^{\MeasurementWindowEnd} \DevicePower(\TimeInstant) \, d\TimeInstant\] A calibrated power analyzer can integrate accurately even when its exported log is slow, because the instrument samples voltage and current internally at high frequency and accumulates their product in hardware between log intervals. Polling an unaccumulated instantaneous power reading at a low rate, by contrast, can alias rapid swings between compute-bound and memory-bound phases. For a steady-state energy comparison, the harness separates initialization from the timed workload through three protocol stages:

  1. A warm-up phase runs unmeasured inferences until caches, buffers, and temperature reach the intended operating state.
  2. A sustained measurement window executes repeated inferences across minutes to average over microarchitectural phase shifts and thermal drift.
  3. Hardware-to-software synchronization aligns power and workload timestamps so integrated energy and completed requests cover the same window. Cold-start or intermittent deployments require additional measurements that include initialization, sleep, and wake-up costs.

Workload structure further alters the power profile. In low-batch Transformer execution, decode often has less weight reuse than prefill and can face a lower bandwidth ceiling; their measured power profiles depend on shapes, kernels, and operating policy. DLRM embedding lookups can make memory traffic a substantial energy contributor, which must be measured alongside dense computation and static power. Batch sizing creates another trade-off: larger batches amortize parameter reads over more inputs and can lower energy per inference, while also changing activation traffic, execution time, and instantaneous power. At the edge, the duty cycle governs the outcome through the duty-cycle average power of section 12.7.2, where the keyword-spotting case shows the idle floor setting battery life.

12.7.5 MLPerf power case study

MLPerf Power (Tschand et al. 2024) translates raw electrical measurements into standardized efficiency metrics: completed inferences per second per watt under a rigorously defined system boundary. This protocol applies uniform measurement principles across data center, edge, and tiny inference scenarios, where the binding constraint shifts from rack cooling and operating cost to battery runtime and microwatt energy harvesting.

Standardizing the boundary prevents misleading comparisons across architectural families. By specifying instrumentation, measurement boundaries, matched logging windows, and the applicable workload constraints, MLPerf Power makes efficiency results interpretable within the tested scenario.

Over successive benchmark submission rounds from 2021 to 2024, standardized measurements capture how architectural improvements, compiler optimizations, and lower-precision arithmetic translate into production efficiency gains. Tracking normalized energy efficiency (samples per joule) across eight submission cycles from v1.0 to v4.1, figure 11 charts diverging efficiency trajectories across seven distinct MLPerf Inference workloads.

RetinaNet and ResNet show the largest plotted gains, each reaching 3\(\times\) its initial normalized efficiency. BERT and GPT-J reach about 1.9\(\times\), while DLRM-v2 reaches about 1.7\(\times\), Llama 2 about 1.5\(\times\), and RNN-T about 1.3\(\times\). These trajectories combine changes in hardware, software, numerical formats, and submitted configurations. Static convolutional shapes can benefit from supported low-precision kernels and fusion; low-batch decoding and sparse embedding lookups often give memory improvements greater importance. The plotted ratios alone do not isolate how much each mechanism contributed.

Timing protocols and power instrumentation provide the raw data for benchmarking. Raw data alone, however, does not guarantee sound conclusions. Converting measurements into meaningful comparisons requires understanding the systematic sources of error, bias, and misalignment that can make even carefully collected benchmark numbers misleading.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A vendor advertises an AI accelerator as delivering ‘\(10\text{ TOPS}\) at \(0.5\text{ W}\)’ from a core-only measurement, while a matched measurement of the same workload on the full deployed system shows \(4.5\text{ W}\) of active power at the wall socket. What benchmarking issue most directly explains the difference?

    1. The vendor drew an isolated power measurement boundary around the compute core silicon only, omitting DRAM interfaces, PCIe host transfers, voltage regulators, CPU preprocessing, and cooling fans
    2. The electrical wall outlet was defective and provided improper alternating current
    3. Power consumption in digital circuits is inherently non-deterministic and varies by \(10\times\) between runs
    4. The vendor measured power during sleep mode rather than active compute

  2. Assume switching activity and capacitance stay fixed. An accelerator raises clock frequency by \(5\%\), and useful inference throughput also rises by \(5\%\), but doing so requires a \(15\%\) increase in supply voltage. Using the dynamic-power model \(P \propto V^2 f\), the dynamic power rises by about \(1.15^2 \times 1.05 \approx 1.389\) (\(38.9\%\)). What follows for dynamic performance per watt at this operating point in a power-constrained edge deployment?

    1. It is an optimal trade-off because throughput is always the only metric that matters
    2. It represents a severe dynamic energy-efficiency regression, reducing dynamic performance-per-watt to about \(0.756\) of baseline
    3. Performance-per-watt increases because higher frequency reduces static leakage
    4. The device will operate cooler because inferences finish \(5\%\) sooner

  3. Explain why one instantaneous power reading during an ML inference workload produces misleading results, and describe how standardized protocols calculate total energy.

  4. True or False: In standardized ML power benchmarking, measuring the power draw of the arithmetic compute units (ALUs and tensor cores) is sufficient because the energy required to read and write data from DRAM is negligible in comparison.

  5. Order the following steps in an illustrative steady-state ML power measurement workflow from start to finish, where this protocol records an idle baseline before warm-up and then measures a matched active window:

  1. Integrate instantaneous power readings over the matched active measurement window (\(E = \int P(t) \, dt\)) and compute Joules per inference
  2. Establish the physical measurement boundary and connect a calibrated power analyzer to the appropriate measurement points
  3. Execute unmeasured warm-up iterations until the device achieves thermal equilibrium (steady-state junction temperature)
  4. Measure and record the baseline idle/quiescent power consumption while the system is waiting for requests
  5. Execute the synchronized inference benchmark workload while logging power over the matched measurement window

12.8 Benchmarking Best Practices

Those sources of error, bias, and misalignment explain why an inference stack that passes a steady-state MLPerf run in the lab can still miss its latency target when production traffic arrives in correlated bursts, or why a two-point leaderboard gain can sit entirely inside test-set sampling noise. Training throughput, inference latency, and power efficiency each have established measurement protocols, yet those protocols rely on simplifying assumptions that diverge from production reality.

Training suites freeze datasets and specify randomness even though production data and retraining conditions evolve continuously. Inference suites emphasize a defined steady-state scenario even though live traffic arrives in bursts with variable sequence lengths. Power benchmarks control thermal environments where deployed accelerators instead face variable ambient temperatures and sustained thermal loads. Turning raw measurements into trustworthy engineering decisions requires guarding against four classes of failure, namely statistical and methodological noise, irreproducible run envelopes, system and deployment misalignment (including the hardware lottery), and organizational incentives to game the score.

12.8.1 Statistical and methodological issues

A one-point accuracy drop on 1,000 test images or a 2 percent latency improvement between single benchmark runs can sit entirely inside sampling and system noise. Telling a genuine change from a fluke requires the same statistical apparatus for every benchmark in the chapter, from a kernel timing to an accuracy comparison.

To isolate run-to-run variability, benchmark protocols require repeated experimental trials across controlled pseudo-random seeds. The number of runs must satisfy explicit statistical-power targets, specifying the probability of detecting a stated real effect, rather than opportunistic sampling. Reporting must extend beyond single best-of-\(\RepeatedRunCount\) scores or isolated arithmetic means. Reporting sample variance, standard deviations, or confidence intervals quantifies stability and establishes whether an observed speedup exceeds background system jitter. Without these controls, benchmarking yields misleading conclusions. In reinforcement learning, reported performance advantages frequently evaporate into the statistical noise of random weight initializations and environment dynamics (Henderson et al. 2018). Similarly, unstandardized evaluations of generative adversarial networks produce rank reversals across random seeds, demonstrating that uncharacterized variance undermines comparative claims (Lucic et al. 2018).

Lucic, Mario, Karol Kurach, Marcin Michalski, Sylvain Gelly, and Olivier Bousquet. 2018. “Are GANs Created Equal? A Large-Scale Study.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 31.

Even when run-to-run execution variance is fully controlled, benchmarking encounters a structural constraint in the evaluation dataset itself: test-set measurement capacity. A test set operates as a measurement instrument with a finite resolution limit. Because accuracy on a finite sample is a binomial proportion, empirical scores carry intrinsic sampling variance that scales inversely with sample size. When an optimization alters accuracy by one or two percentage points, the sample size and paired pattern of disagreements determine whether the test can distinguish a genuine algorithmic effect from sampling fluctuation. As analyzed in the accompanying notebook, marginal accuracy scores on a thousand-sample test set are insufficient to establish the significance of a fine-grained difference without examining the paired outcomes.

At most 26 disagreements reveal a regression; the disagreement count decides.

Napkin Math 12.5: The statistical confidence trap
Problem: A baseline classifier scores 95 percent, while a compressed version scores 94 percent on the same 1,000 images. Does this one-point drop establish a regression?

Math:

  1. Expected errors: The scores correspond to 50 errors and 60 errors. Under the baseline binomial model, the error count has a standard deviation of about 7 errors.

  2. Difference interval (95 percent): The same-image comparison requires paired outcomes, which the two marginal scores do not supply. For comparison, suppose the models instead achieved these scores on independent test sets sampled from the same population. Let \(\EstimatedAccuracy_1\) and \(\EstimatedAccuracy_2\) denote their accuracy estimates, and let \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) be the number of images evaluated for each model. Under that independent-set assumption, the standard error of their difference is

    \[ \operatorname{SE}(\EstimatedAccuracy_1-\EstimatedAccuracy_2) = \sqrt{\frac{\EstimatedAccuracy_1(1-\EstimatedAccuracy_1)}{\EvaluationSampleCount} + \frac{\EstimatedAccuracy_2(1-\EstimatedAccuracy_2)}{\EvaluationSampleCount}}. \]

    The observed 1 percentage point difference has an approximate interval from -1 percentage point to 3 percentage points, which includes zero. Treated as independent samples, the one-point drop is indistinguishable from sampling noise. Pairing can change that verdict, because images that both models classify the same way cancel out of the comparison.

  3. Paired test on the same images: The stated problem uses one test set, so the decisive evidence is where the two models disagree. Let \(\BaselineOnlyCorrectCount\) count images only the baseline classifies correctly and \(\CompressedOnlyCorrectCount\) count images only the compressed model classifies correctly. Images both models get right or both get wrong carry no information about which is better, and McNemar’s test (McNemar 1947) discards them. Because the compressed model makes 10 more errors overall than the baseline, every image that only the compressed model gets right must be offset by one more image that only the baseline gets right. The marginal scores therefore fix the net difference \(\BaselineOnlyCorrectCount- \CompressedOnlyCorrectCount\) at 10, while leaving the total disagreement count \(\BaselineOnlyCorrectCount+ \CompressedOnlyCorrectCount\) free to vary. The test statistic, without continuity correction, is

    \[ \McnemarTestStatistic= \frac{(\BaselineOnlyCorrectCount- \CompressedOnlyCorrectCount)^2}{\BaselineOnlyCorrectCount+ \CompressedOnlyCorrectCount}, \]

    and the drop is significant at 95 percent when \(\McnemarTestStatistic\) exceeds 3.84. That happens only when the models disagree on at most 26 images, for example when the compressed model makes the same 50 errors as the baseline plus 10 new ones (\(\BaselineOnlyCorrectCount = 10, \CompressedOnlyCorrectCount = 0\)).

    Implication: The marginal scores cannot answer the question; the disagreement count decides it. If the compressed model’s 10 extra errors are nearly its only disagreements with the baseline, the paired test supplies evidence of a regression at the stated significance threshold. If the models disagree on many more images, the test leaves the same one-point gap unresolved. Nonsignificance does not establish equivalence or compliance with a permitted quality-loss tolerance. A benchmark report must therefore keep per-image outcomes, not only the two accuracies; the interval-based decision rule below separates an unresolved difference from a supported improvement.

Systems insight: A small test set limits what a benchmark can resolve. The test set, viewed as a measurement instrument, must be sized to match the precision of the change it is meant to detect. A single accuracy estimate alone needs about 1,825 images for ±1 percentage point precision, and that sample size does not by itself power a comparison between two models.

McNemar, Quinn. 1947. “Note on the Sampling Error of the Difference Between Correlated Proportions or Percentages.” Psychometrika 12 (2): 153–57. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02295996.

The same paired logic governs timing comparisons. Suppose an engineer measures baseline and candidate mean latency under the same request mix, offered load, and timing boundary. Each experimental trial pairs a complete baseline run with a complete candidate run, following the same warm-up and thermal-state protocol and randomizing their execution order to limit drift bias. The comparison assumes that separately repeated run pairs are independent, with persistent background load or thermal drift controlled or accounted for. Requests within one run can share queues, caches, and thermal conditions and therefore cannot automatically count as independent trials. The report states the interval method and confidence level and uses the run pairs as the repeated observations. A request-latency p99 describes the slow end of the observed request population. It is neither a confidence interval for mean latency nor a measure of uncertainty in the estimated p99.

Suppose the report supplies an uncertainty interval for the mean latency reduction, defined as baseline latency minus candidate latency. If the interval extends from a negative reduction to a positive reduction, it includes no change, and the experiment cannot resolve whether the candidate is faster. That conclusion does not establish that the implementations perform identically. If a subsequent adequately controlled comparison yields an interval entirely above zero, it supports a latency reduction under the tested conditions. Deployment still requires a prespecified minimum useful reduction, reflecting the cost of adopting the candidate. An interval entirely above zero but extending below that threshold supports a gain without establishing that the gain justifies adoption; an interval entirely above the threshold supports both claims, provided the quality and resource gates also pass.

Two further issues undermine reliability even when runs are repeated and compared correctly. Incomplete problem coverage occurs when a benchmark exercises only a narrow operating point of a system. Controlled datasets such as CIFAR-10 (Krizhevsky 2009) constrain inputs to uniform, low-resolution images (\(32{\times}32\) pixels) with small per-image input footprints. This constrained input shape may exercise memory differently from production images, but the complete working set still depends on the model and batch size, and allocation or kernel-launch overheads remain. A model that achieves high accuracy and throughput on a uniform, downscaled benchmark can stall in deployment when exposed to variable image resolutions, arbitrary aspect ratios, and sensor noise that force frequent kernel recompilations or fallback execution paths.

Krizhevsky, Alex. 2009. Learning Multiple Layers of Features from Tiny Images. University of Toronto.

Statistical insignificance arises when evaluations rely on too few trials to distinguish algorithmic improvements from measurement noise, particularly when the evaluation medium introduces variance. Large language model evaluation exemplifies this vulnerability: whether scoring candidate generations against a reference via human preference ratings or an LLM-as-judge protocol, scores vary substantially based on prompt phrasing, judge model alignment, and response ordering. A reported two-point win can vanish under an alternative prompt template or judge configuration. Rigorous evaluation demands paired hypothesis tests and bootstrap confidence intervals over hundreds of independent prompt pairings. Omitting these confidence bounds obscures whether a reported margin reflects a genuine architectural advance or stochastic evaluation noise.

12.8.2 Reproducibility

Reproducibility studies have repeatedly shown that modest benchmark gains can disappear when random seeds, hardware, framework versions, or implementation details change (Henderson et al. 2018). The stochastic processes that section 12.4.2 identified as measurement noise interact, over a long training run, with hardware-specific behaviors (floating-point rounding, memory layout, compiler optimizations) to produce results that can vary meaningfully across environments.

Henderson, Peter, Riashat Islam, Philip Bachman, Joelle Pineau, Doina Precup, and David Meger. 2018. “Deep Reinforcement Learning That Matters.” Proceedings of the AAAI Conference on Artificial Intelligence 32 (1): 3207–14. https://doi.org/10.1609/aaai.v32i1.11694.

A deeper layer of non-determinism comes from the parallel hardware itself. Operations such as parallel atomic additions, used during gradient accumulation for sparse embeddings in models like Graph Neural Networks, execute in non-deterministic order across threads when concurrent updates target the same memory location. The resulting floating-point summation order changes across runs, and because floating-point addition is not associative (Reproducibility and system artifacts), a different order produces bit-for-bit different gradients even with identical inputs and seeds. Enforcing bit-exact reproducibility in these cases requires a deterministic reduction path, which can reduce training throughput, a direct trade-off between reproducibility and performance that benchmark protocols must explicitly address. Without explicit controls for all these sources of variability, benchmark numbers reflect a specific confluence of conditions rather than a system’s genuine capability.

MLPerf Training addresses this through standardized data preprocessing, target-quality rules, and repeated accepted runs that characterize stochastic variation (Mattson et al. 2020). The aim is to show that the reported performance reflects system capability rather than a favorable combination of stochastic factors, which a single fast run cannot show.

For a training benchmark, reproducibility is therefore the full run envelope, not the random seed alone. A credible report must preserve the model commit, dataset checksum, preprocessing pipeline, seed plan, framework and compiler versions, precision policy, batch schedule, hardware topology, thermal and power limits, and checkpoint behavior. It must also report the distribution of accepted runs rather than a single best run. Only then can the benchmark separate a real system improvement from a favorable interaction among software version, hardware state, and stochastic training path.

Reproducibility also limits comparisons across software environments. A compiler or library revision can change kernel tiling, memory access, and reduction order, so an unexplained version change is a second intervention in the experiment. The run envelope above must accompany the result; matching a model name and seed alone does not make two stacks equivalent.

12.8.3 System design challenges

Even a statistically significant, bit-for-bit reproducible benchmark fails if it measures the wrong operational regime, as when a serving stack that hits high QPS in Offline mode or under smooth Poisson Server arrivals violates its latency SLO once correlated production bursts or long-context prompts fill its queues and memory. Misalignment with real-world goals persists even when a standardized benchmark is executed and reproduced cleanly, because Offline and Server scenarios answer different questions and the Server scenario specifies only one arrival distribution. The experiment must state which operating conditions its conclusion covers, and production qualification tests those boundaries before release; the summary returns to these gaps as a release problem once model and data evaluation are in place.

Beyond deployment alignment, a further category of limitations stems directly from the physical hardware executing the workload. Accelerator behavior fluctuates with thermal conditions, operating system resource contention, and underlying microarchitectural topology.

Environmental controls must match the claim being tested. Fixed clocks, CPU affinity, and isolated memory allocations can reduce noise in a comparison of implementations, while a deployment test must retain the relevant contention and thermal policy. The sustained-state mechanisms in section 12.6.4 apply to servers as well as edge devices. A result obtained under tighter isolation supports an implementation comparison, but does not by itself establish production capacity.

The hardware lottery28 (Hooker 2021) compounds this physical variation by coupling algorithmic viability to specialized hardware support. Modern accelerators allocate most of their silicon area to dense matrix multiplication units (such as Tensor Cores or systolic arrays) backed by wide vector units and high-bandwidth memory hierarchies. Algorithmic architectures such as dense Transformers achieve high arithmetic intensity by reusing weights across batched tokens, sustaining a large fraction of peak theoretical FLOP/s. Conversely, architectures that rely on dynamic control flow, fine-grained sparsity, or graph message passing suffer from uncoalesced memory transactions and thread divergence, underutilizing available compute silicon. Algorithmic performance on a benchmark often reflects how naturally a model’s computation graph maps onto dominant accelerator execution units rather than intrinsic mathematical superiority. The lottery is how the bitter lesson shows up on a leaderboard. General methods win by riding growth in computation, but the computation that grows is the dense form the dominant accelerators supply, so the winning methods are also the ones shaped to fit it.

28 Hardware lottery: Coined by Hooker (2021) to describe how algorithmic success depends on alignment with available hardware and software. Transformer workloads benefit from dense matrix operations that map efficiently to modern accelerators, while graph neural networks and sparse mixture-of-experts models can be harder to evaluate when available silicon and software stacks favor dense kernels. Hardware-specific leaderboards therefore favor architectures aligned with the measured platform and may obscure alternatives that would fare differently under other hardware assumptions.

Hooker, Sara. 2021. “The Hardware Lottery.” Communications of the ACM 64 (12): 58–65. https://doi.org/10.1145/3467017.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, that general methods which scale with computation outrun hand-built structure, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

Hardware compatibility dependence introduces systematic biases when evaluating models across heterogeneous execution targets. An architecture optimized for GPU execution may underperform on an embedded CPU, DSP, or custom edge accelerator with differing vector widths, cache hierarchies, and quantization support. Across five hardware targets on a Google Pixel 4, figure 12 charts how the latency–accuracy Pareto frontier reshuffles between CPU float, CPU uint8, GPU, EdgeTPU, and DSP execution. The diagonal purple arrow links MobileNetV3 Large at roughly 75 percent top-1 accuracy, from approximately 20 ms on CPU float to 4.5 ms on the DSP. Multi-MAX reaches roughly 75 percent accuracy at 2.2 ms on EdgeTPU and 3.4 ms on DSP; its points at higher accuracy carry higher latency. Comparisons must therefore match both the model variant and the accuracy operating point. The shifted frontiers show why model selection requires measurements on the target execution path rather than a latency ranking inherited from another processor.

Without benchmarking across heterogeneous hardware configurations, system designers risk selecting architectures solely because they conform to existing accelerator memory hierarchies and instruction sets. Evaluating models across diverse execution engines ensures that architectural decisions reflect computational efficiency rather than platform-specific co-design artifacts.

12.8.4 Organizational and strategic issues

Statistical power, reproducible run envelopes, and target-matched hardware tests all address measurement mechanics. A distinct category of failure comes from human incentives, because when a multimillion-dollar procurement contract or a paper acceptance hinges on a single leaderboard rank, teams face strong pressure to optimize for the score itself rather than for representative deployment performance.

Benchmark engineering

While the hardware lottery is an unintended consequence of hardware specialization, benchmark engineering is the deliberate practice of tailoring models or systems to exploit the idiosyncrasies of specific benchmark harnesses. This practice yields inflated performance metrics that collapse under production conditions.

Benchmark engineering occurs when developers tune hyperparameters, preprocessing pipelines, or execution paths specifically to maximize benchmark scores rather than generalizable performance. Legitimate optimization respects the run rules and declared workload; misleading benchmark engineering exploits restricted test conditions while making broader claims that the evidence cannot support. For example, an inference pipeline might hardcode input tensor dimensions and pad batches specifically to match the memory stride of an accelerator’s GEMM tile configuration. Such a pipeline achieves peak throughput on fixed benchmark shapes while triggering costly kernel fallbacks and memory reallocations on arbitrary production inputs. Similarly, fine-tuning an evaluation pipeline to match the formatting biases of an automated evaluation model produces high leaderboard rankings that fail when interacting with unstructured human dialogue.

Procurement contracts and marketing claims reward exactly this tuning. It is Goodhart’s Law (section 12.1) acting on an organization rather than on a single team’s choice of metric.

To keep this benchmark gaming from corrupting procurement and engineering evaluations, standardized suites enforce strict run rules backed by reference implementations, submission audits, and division boundaries. Their rules forbid three classes of non-generalizable shortcuts:

  1. Precision truncation without disclosure: A compiler silently lowers numerical precision (for instance, executing intermediate activations in FP8 or INT8 instead of BF16) to inflate arithmetic throughput, without disclosing the reduced dynamic range or verifying whether the lower precision degrades numerical stability on production workloads.
  2. Work pruning and operator elimination: An execution engine exploits fixed benchmark test sets to skip operations required by general production inputs, such as bypassing dynamic padding removal, masking out-of-bounds tokens, or skipping input boundary validation.
  3. Benchmark fingerprinting: A runtime inspects input tensor dimensions, dataset hash fingerprints, or memory layout patterns to identify the benchmark suite, executing hardcoded, pre-tuned execution graphs and bespoke kernel configurations that are inaccessible to arbitrary user workloads.

MLPerf detects and deters these shortcuts through its submission divisions (defined in section 12.4.4), reference-equivalence checks, and peer-reviewed code audits. The closed division permits reproducible runtime optimizations, memory layout transformations (such as converting NCHW to NHWC), hardware-generalizable kernel fusion, and approved low-precision formats with documented, verifiable output quality, while non-generalizable shortcuts remain prohibited. Broader architectural alterations belong in the open division.

Bias and over-optimization

System engineers evaluating benchmark results must distinguish legitimate architectural gains from benchmark engineering, and several practices expose the difference. Submissions that publish complete compiler flags, kernel configurations, and execution traces let independent evaluators separate general efficiency gains from harness-specific tuning. Paired results from controlled benchmarks and uncurated production traces expose discrepancies in latency distributions and memory use. Evaluating across several independently maintained suites raises the barrier against overfitting, because an optimization tailored to the quirks of one harness rarely transfers to others.

Third-party verification adds accountability. Independent audits check whether reported speedups reproduce on identical hardware, as in MLPerf’s reference-versus-submission validation (section 12.8.4.1), and application-specific stress tests probe failure modes that static benchmarks omit, such as vision models under adverse imaging conditions or inference engines under skewed request traffic.

Benchmark evolution

Benchmarks are not static standards; they must evolve alongside model architectures and hardware capabilities. A benchmark that effectively separates system capabilities in one architectural generation becomes obsolete when models saturate its accuracy ceiling or when hardware innovations shift the primary system bottleneck. Stale benchmarks incentivize over-optimizing legacy computational kernels rather than advancing system efficiency.

Early deep learning benchmarks evaluated image classification and object detection on convolutional networks. As workloads expanded to large language models, recommendation systems, and generative AI, those vision benchmarks failed to capture long-context attention, autoregressive decoding, and sparse expert routing, and community benchmarks moved to language understanding (Wang et al. 2018, 2019) and foundation model capabilities (Liang et al. 2022). How saturation erodes a static benchmark’s discriminating power is a data question, taken up in section 12.9.2.

Wang, Alex, Yada Pruksachatkun, Nikita Nangia, Amanpreet Singh, Julian Michael, Felix Hill, Omer Levy, and Samuel R. Bowman. 2019. “SuperGLUE: A Stickier Benchmark for General-Purpose Language Understanding Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1905.00537.

Benchmark evolution also expands the dimensions of measurement. While early benchmarks focused on throughput and accuracy, contemporary systems require multi-objective evaluation across energy efficiency, memory footprint, and tail latency under resource constraints. Scientific instruments provide a further constraint regime in figure 13. Its shaded “Fast ML for Science” envelope places high-throughput trigger pipelines outside typical mobile and IoT operating points (Duarte et al. 2022). The distance between these operating points is why a benchmark must match its application.

Managing benchmark evolution introduces a direct trade-off between longitudinal stability and architectural relevance. Benchmarks must remain fixed long enough to allow rigorous cross-generation hardware and software comparisons. If benchmarks change too frequently, tracking historical progress becomes impossible. Conversely, freezing a benchmark indefinitely leads to metric stagnation and over-specialization. Modern benchmarking consortia balance this trade-off by operating on fixed release cycles that introduce new representative workloads while deprecating obsolete suites.

Even the most rigorous system benchmarks validate only one dimension of deployment readiness. Achieving record throughput and energy efficiency on an MLPerf workload confirms that the hardware and runtime stack can deliver high computational efficiency. It cannot determine whether the deployed model generalizes to real-world distributions or whether the underlying dataset represents the operational population. In the D·A·M taxonomy, hardware throughput (Machine) is a necessary foundation, but algorithmic fidelity (Algorithm) and dataset integrity (Data) must hold simultaneously. Validation therefore continues with the model and the data.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An image classification model achieves \(95\%\) accuracy on the CIFAR-10 benchmark test set, but when deployed on a mobile robot operating in a warehouse, its accuracy drops to \(70\%\). Assume the warehouse images are verified to use the same class definitions and preprocessing contract but differ substantially in lighting, motion blur, and camera noise. Which benchmarking limitation most plausibly explains the gap?

    1. The mobile robot CPU lacked 64-bit floating-point registers
    2. The CIFAR-10 evaluation used too few random seeds during training
    3. Incomplete benchmark coverage and distributional narrowness: the benchmark dataset contained clean, centered, low-resolution images that failed to represent warehouse camera noise, lighting variations, and motion blur
    4. The benchmark harness executed the test set in the wrong order

  2. Which set of governance and methodological rules does the MLPerf consortium implement to discourage and detect benchmark gaming through benchmark-specific shortcuts?

    1. Allowing submitters to create proprietary synthetic test datasets that are kept secret from competitors
    2. Permitting compilers to silently lower numerical precision below IEEE standards without reporting the accuracy impact
    3. Evaluating systems solely on peak theoretical arithmetic operations per second without measuring execution time
    4. Enforcing strict Closed Division rules (requiring reference-equivalent workloads under defined allowances), prohibiting benchmark detection code branching, and requiring open peer-review log audits

  3. What is the core insight of the ‘Hardware Lottery’ concept (coined by Sara Hooker in 2021) regarding the relationship between ML benchmarks and research progress?

    1. An algorithmic research idea often succeeds not because it is universally superior, but because existing hardware accelerators and software compilers happen to be highly optimized for its specific computational pattern (such as dense GEMM)
    2. Hardware performance is purely random and cannot be measured with scientific accuracy
    3. Researchers should purchase computer hardware using randomized government lotteries
    4. Deep neural networks perform identically across all hardware architectures regardless of compiler support

  4. True or False: If a benchmark measurement is conducted with flawless statistical rigor—using 1,000 independent runs, narrow confidence intervals, and controlled thermal states—its results are guaranteed to predict real-world production system performance.

  5. The structural phenomenon where a machine learning algorithm achieves prominence primarily because specialized hardware and software compilers were already co-optimized for its execution pattern is called the ____.

  6. Explain the fundamental tension between benchmark stability and benchmark evolution, and describe how benchmark consortia manage this trade-off.
